The Homeric hero to whom Agamemnon offers seven cities' sovereignty for his friendship.

## modern-en · hesiod · 30:161

In every age and country of the world, people must have paid attention to one another's characters, intentions, and actions, and many respectable rules and maxims for the conduct of human life must have been laid down and approved by common consent. As soon as writing came into fashion, wise men—or those who fancied themselves such—would naturally try to add to the stock of these established and respected maxims, and to set down their own sense of what counted as proper or improper conduct, sometimes in the more elaborate form of fables, like those called the fables of Aesop, and sometimes in the simpler form of apophthegms or wise sayings, like the proverbs of Solomon, the verses of Theognis and Phocyllides, and part of the works of Hesiod. They might go on this way for a long time, merely multiplying the number of these maxims of prudence and morality, without even trying to arrange them in any very distinct or methodical order, much less to link them together under one or more general principles from which they could all be derived, like effects from their natural causes. The beauty of arranging different observations systematically, connected by a few common principles, was first glimpsed in those crude early attempts at a system of natural philosophy. Something of the same kind was afterwards attempted in morals. The maxims of everyday life were set in some methodical order and tied together by a few common principles, just as people had tried to arrange and connect the phenomena of nature. The science that claims to investigate and explain these connecting principles is what is properly called Moral Philosophy.

Named among the early Greek didactic poets whose work circulated before formal schools of philosophy existed.

## modern-en · polybius · 30:176

In ancient Rome, the exercises of the Campus Martius served the same purpose as those of the Gymnasium in ancient Greece, and they seem to have served it equally well. But among the Romans there was nothing corresponding to the musical education of the Greeks. Even so, the morals of the Romans, in both private and public life, seem to have been not merely equal but, on the whole, a good deal superior to those of the Greeks. That they were superior in private life we have on the express testimony of Polybius and of Dionysius of Halicarnassus, two authors well acquainted with both nations; and the whole course of Greek and Roman history bears witness to the superiority of Roman public morals. The good temper and moderation of rival factions seem to be the most essential feature of the public morals of a free people. But the factions of the Greeks were almost always violent and bloody, whereas until the time of the Gracchi no blood had ever been shed in any Roman faction; and from the time of the Gracchi the Roman republic may be regarded as in reality dissolved. So, despite the very respectable authority of Plato, Aristotle, and Polybius, and despite the very ingenious reasons by which Montesquieu tries to support that authority, it seems likely that the musical education of the Greeks did little to improve their morals, since without any such education those of the Romans were, on the whole, superior. The respect of those ancient sages for the institutions of their ancestors had probably inclined them to find much political wisdom in what was perhaps merely an ancient custom, carried on without interruption from the earliest period of those societies down to the times when they had reached a considerable degree of refinement. Music and dancing are the great amusements of almost all barbarous nations, and the great accomplishments thought to fit a man for entertaining his company. It is so to this day among the negroes on the coast of Africa. It was so among the ancient Celts, among the ancient Scandinavians, and, as we learn from Homer, among the ancient Greeks in the times before the Trojan war. When the Greek tribes had formed themselves into little republics, it was natural that the study of these accomplishments should for a long time form part of the public and common education of the people.

Cited, with Dionysius of Halicarnassus, for direct testimony on Roman moral superiority over the Greeks in private life, and named with Plato and Aristotle as a respectable authority.

## modern-en · thucydides · 30:6

An army of hunters can rarely exceed two or three hundred men. The unreliable food supply that hunting provides could seldom let a larger number stay together for any length of time. An army of shepherds, by contrast, may sometimes reach two or three hundred thousand. As long as nothing blocks their advance, as long as they can keep moving from one district whose forage they have used up to another that is still untouched, there seems to be almost no limit to how many can march together. A nation of hunters can never threaten the civilized nations near it; a nation of shepherds can. Nothing is more contemptible than an Indian war in North America; nothing, on the other hand, is more terrifying than the Tartar invasions that have so often struck Asia. Thucydides judged that neither Europe nor Asia could resist the Scythians if they united, and the experience of every age has borne this out. The peoples of the vast but defenceless plains of Scythia or Tartary have often been brought together under the rule of some conquering horde's or clan's chief, and their union has always been marked by the havoc and devastation of Asia. The peoples of the inhospitable deserts of Arabia, the other great shepherd nation, have been united only once, under Muhammad and his immediate successors. Their union, which owed more to religious zeal than to conquest, was marked in the same way. If the hunting nations of America ever became shepherds, their nearness would be far more dangerous to the European colonies than it is now.

Cited for the judgment that Europe and Asia together could not resist a Scythian invasion, and for observing the Peloponnesians' seasonal pattern of campaigning.

## modern-en · lucian · 30:179

As refinement advanced and philosophy and rhetoric came into fashion, the better sort of people would send their children to the schools of philosophers and rhetoricians to be instructed in these fashionable subjects. But those schools were not supported by the public; for a long time they were barely tolerated by it. The demand for philosophy and rhetoric was for a long time so small that the first professional teachers of either could not find steady employment in any one city and were obliged to travel from place to place. This was how Zeno of Elea, Protagoras, Gorgias, Hippias, and many others lived. As demand grew, the schools of both philosophy and rhetoric settled in one place, first in Athens and afterwards in several other cities. The state, however, seems never to have encouraged them beyond assigning some of them a particular place to teach in—something that was sometimes done by private donors as well. The state seems to have assigned the Academy to Plato, the Lyceum to Aristotle, and the Portico to Zeno of Citium, the founder of the Stoics. But Epicurus left his gardens to his own school. Until about the time of Marcus Antoninus, however, no teacher appears to have drawn any salary from the public or to have had any earnings beyond the honoraria or fees of his pupils. The bounty that this philosophical emperor, as we learn from Lucian, bestowed on one of the teachers of philosophy probably lasted no longer than his own life. There was nothing equivalent to the privileges of graduation, and attending any of these schools was not required in order to practise any particular trade or profession. If the belief in their own usefulness could not draw students to them, the law neither forced anyone to attend them nor rewarded anyone for having done so. The teachers had no jurisdiction over their pupils and no authority beyond the natural authority that superior virtue and ability never fail to win from young people toward those entrusted with any part of their education.

Cited for reporting the salary bestowed by "that philosophical emperor" (Marcus Antoninus) on a teacher of philosophy.

## modern-en · marcus-antoninus · 30:179

As refinement advanced and philosophy and rhetoric came into fashion, the better sort of people would send their children to the schools of philosophers and rhetoricians to be instructed in these fashionable subjects. But those schools were not supported by the public; for a long time they were barely tolerated by it. The demand for philosophy and rhetoric was for a long time so small that the first professional teachers of either could not find steady employment in any one city and were obliged to travel from place to place. This was how Zeno of Elea, Protagoras, Gorgias, Hippias, and many others lived. As demand grew, the schools of both philosophy and rhetoric settled in one place, first in Athens and afterwards in several other cities. The state, however, seems never to have encouraged them beyond assigning some of them a particular place to teach in—something that was sometimes done by private donors as well. The state seems to have assigned the Academy to Plato, the Lyceum to Aristotle, and the Portico to Zeno of Citium, the founder of the Stoics. But Epicurus left his gardens to his own school. Until about the time of Marcus Antoninus, however, no teacher appears to have drawn any salary from the public or to have had any earnings beyond the honoraria or fees of his pupils. The bounty that this philosophical emperor, as we learn from Lucian, bestowed on one of the teachers of philosophy probably lasted no longer than his own life. There was nothing equivalent to the privileges of graduation, and attending any of these schools was not required in order to practise any particular trade or profession. If the belief in their own usefulness could not draw students to them, the law neither forced anyone to attend them nor rewarded anyone for having done so. The teachers had no jurisdiction over their pupils and no authority beyond the natural authority that superior virtue and ability never fail to win from young people toward those entrusted with any part of their education.

"That philosophical emperor," the first, per Smith, to endow a salaried teaching chair at Athens.

## modern-en · epictetus · 30:238

It may perhaps be worth remarking that, if we leave out the poets, a few orators, and a few historians, by far the greater part of the other eminent men of letters of both Greece and Rome appear to have been either public or private teachers, generally either of philosophy or of rhetoric. This will be found to hold true from the days of Lysias and Isocrates, of Plato and Aristotle, down to those of Plutarch and Epictetus, Suetonius and Quintilian. To impose on a man the necessity of teaching, year after year, in some particular branch of science seems in fact to be the most effective method for making him completely master of it himself. By being obliged to go over the same ground every year, he necessarily becomes, in a few years, if he is good for anything, well acquainted with every part of it; and if on some particular point he has formed too hasty an opinion one year, when in the course of his lectures he comes to reconsider the same subject the next year, he is very likely to correct it. Just as being a teacher of science is the natural employment of a mere man of letters, so it is also perhaps the training most likely to make him a man of solid learning and knowledge. The moderation of church incomes naturally tends to draw most of the men of letters in a country to the employment in which they can be most useful to the public, and at the same time to give them the best education they are perhaps capable of receiving. It tends to make their learning both as solid and as useful as possible.

Named with Suetonius and Quintilian among later authors continuing the pattern of philosopher-teachers who also wrote.

## modern-en · st-dominic · 30:200

In the Church of Rome the diligence and zeal of the lower clergy are kept more alive by the powerful motive of self-interest than perhaps in any established Protestant church. Many of the parish clergy draw a very large part of their living from the people's voluntary offerings—a source of income that confession gives them many chances to increase. The mendicant orders draw their entire living from such offerings. With them it is as with the hussars and light infantry of some armies: no plunder, no pay. The parish clergy are like teachers whose reward depends partly on a salary and partly on the fees they collect from their pupils, and these fees must always depend, more or less, on their diligence and reputation. The mendicant orders are like teachers whose living depends entirely on their own diligence. They are therefore obliged to use every art that can stir the devotion of ordinary people. Machiavelli observed that the founding of the two great mendicant orders of St Dominic and St Francis revived the flagging faith and devotion of the Catholic church in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. In Roman Catholic countries the spirit of devotion is kept up entirely by the monks and by the poorer parish clergy. The great dignitaries of the church, with all the polish of gentlemen and men of the world, and sometimes of learned men too, take care to maintain the necessary discipline over their inferiors, but seldom trouble themselves about instructing the people.

Founder, with St. Francis, of one of the two great mendicant orders whose establishment, per Machiavelli, revived declining religious credit in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries.

Founder of the second of the two great mendicant orders, with St. Dominic.

## modern-en · martin-luther · 30:232

The followers of Luther, together with what is called the Church of England, kept more or less of episcopal government, maintained a hierarchy among the clergy, gave the sovereign the disposal of all the bishoprics and other consistorial benefices in his dominions, and thereby made him the real head of the church; and without depriving the bishop of the right of filling the smaller benefices in his diocese, they admitted and even favoured, for those benefices too, the right of presentation by the sovereign and by all other lay patrons. This system of church government was from the start favourable to peace and good order and to submission to the civil sovereign. It has accordingly never been the occasion of any tumult or civil disturbance in any country where it has once been established. The Church of England in particular has always prided herself, with good reason, on the unimpeachable loyalty of her principles. Under such a government, the clergy naturally try to recommend themselves to the sovereign, the court, and the nobility and gentry of the country, through whose influence they chiefly expect to obtain preferment. They court these patrons sometimes, no doubt, by the basest flattery and toadying, but often, too, by cultivating all those arts that best deserve, and are therefore most likely to win them, the esteem of people of rank and fortune: by their knowledge in all the different branches of useful and ornamental learning, by the decent generosity of their manners, by the sociable good humour of their conversation, and by their avowed contempt for those absurd and hypocritical austerities that fanatics preach and pretend to practise in order to draw on themselves the reverence, and on most men of rank and fortune (who openly admit they do not practise them) the abhorrence, of ordinary people. Such a clergy, however, while they court the higher ranks in this way, are very apt to neglect entirely the means of keeping up their influence and authority with the lower ones. They are listened to, esteemed, and respected by their superiors; but in front of their inferiors they are often unable to defend, effectively and convincingly, their own sober and moderate doctrines against the most ignorant enthusiast who chooses to attack them.

Founder of the reformed tradition, with the Church of England, that Smith says preserved more of the ancient church's ranks and subordination than Calvin's followers did.

## modern-en · zwingli · 30:233

The followers of Zwingli, or more properly those of Calvin, on the contrary, gave the people of each parish the right of electing their own pastor whenever the church became vacant, and at the same time established the most perfect equality among the clergy. The first part of this arrangement, as long as it remained in force, seems to have produced nothing but disorder and confusion, and to have tended equally to corrupt the morals of both the clergy and the people. The second part seems never to have had any effects but thoroughly agreeable ones.

Named (as printed, "Zuinglius") as a reformer whose followers, more properly those of Calvin, bestowed a more level, egalitarian church constitution on each parish.

## modern-en · calvin · 30:233

The followers of Zwingli, or more properly those of Calvin, on the contrary, gave the people of each parish the right of electing their own pastor whenever the church became vacant, and at the same time established the most perfect equality among the clergy. The first part of this arrangement, as long as it remained in force, seems to have produced nothing but disorder and confusion, and to have tended equally to corrupt the morals of both the clergy and the people. The second part seems never to have had any effects but thoroughly agreeable ones.

The reformer whose followers, per Smith, more properly represent the egalitarian parish-level church constitution credited loosely to Zwingli.

## modern-en · thomas-becket · 20:5

In a country with neither foreign trade nor any of the finer manufactures, a great landowner has nothing to exchange for the bulk of his land's produce beyond what feeds the cultivators, so he consumes the whole surplus in lavish hospitality at home. If that surplus is enough to support a hundred or a thousand men, he can use it in no other way than by supporting a hundred or a thousand men. He is therefore always surrounded by a crowd of retainers and dependants who, having nothing to give in return for their keep but being fed entirely by his bounty, must obey him for the same reason that soldiers must obey the prince who pays them. Before commerce and manufactures spread across Europe, the hospitality of the rich and powerful, from the sovereign down to the smallest baron, surpassed anything we can easily imagine today. Westminster Hall was the dining room of William Rufus, and it may often have been none too large for his company. It counted as a mark of splendour in Thomas Becket that he strewed the floor of his hall with fresh hay or rushes in season, so that the knights and squires who could not find seats would not ruin their fine clothes when they sat on the floor to eat. The great Earl of Warwick is said to have fed 30,000 people every day across his various manors; even if that figure is exaggerated, the real number must have been very large to allow for such an exaggeration. Hospitality of nearly the same kind was still practised not many years ago in many parts of the Scottish Highlands. It seems to be common in every nation that knows little of commerce and manufactures. Doctor Pocock reports that he saw an Arabian chief dine in the streets of a town he had come to in order to sell his cattle, inviting every passer-by, even common beggars, to sit down and share his feast.

Cited for his reputed magnificence in strewing his hall's floor with clean hay or rushes in winter, an anecdote on the state of domestic luxury in his time.

## modern-en · christian-ii-denmark · 30:228

The new doctrines succeeded so well almost everywhere that the princes who happened at the time to be on bad terms with the court of Rome were, by means of them, easily able to overturn the church in their own dominions, which, having lost the respect and reverence of the lower ranks of people, could put up scarcely any resistance. The court of Rome had offended some of the smaller princes in northern Germany, whom it had probably thought too insignificant to be worth humouring. So they all, without exception, established the Reformation in their dominions. The tyranny of Christian II and of Troll, archbishop of Uppsala, enabled Gustavus Vasa to drive them both out of Sweden. The pope favoured the tyrant and the archbishop, and Gustavus Vasa found no difficulty in establishing the Reformation in Sweden. Christian II was afterwards deposed from the throne of Denmark, where his conduct had made him as hated as in Sweden. The pope, however, was still inclined to favour him; and Frederick of Holstein, who had taken the throne in his place, took his revenge by following Gustavus Vasa's example. The magistrates of Berne and Zurich, who had no particular quarrel with the pope, established the Reformation with great ease in their respective cantons, where just beforehand some of the clergy had, by an imposture rather grosser than usual, made the whole order both hateful and contemptible.

The tyrant (printed "Christiern II") whose oppression, with Archbishop Troll's, enabled Gustavus Vasa to expel them from Sweden; later deposed from the throne of Denmark himself.

Archbishop of Uppsala (printed "Upsal"), whose tyranny, with Christian II's, enabled Gustavus Vasa's success in Sweden.

## modern-en · william-conqueror · 4:7

The first public stamps of this kind affixed to the metals in circulation seem in many cases to have been intended to certify what was both the hardest and the most important thing to certify—namely, the goodness or fineness of the metal. They seem to have resembled the sterling mark now stamped on silver plate and bars, or the Spanish mark sometimes stamped on ingots of gold, which, being struck on only one side of the piece and not covering the whole surface, certifies the fineness but not the weight of the metal. Abraham weighs out to Ephron the four hundred shekels of silver he had agreed to pay for the field of Machpelah. They are said, however, to be the merchant's current money, and yet they are received by weight, not by count, in just the same way that ingots of gold and bars of silver are at present. The revenues of the ancient Saxon kings of England are said to have been paid not in money but in kind—that is, in food and provisions of all sorts. William the Conqueror introduced the custom of paying them in money. This money, however, was for a long time received at the exchequer by weight, not by count.

Introduced the custom of paying royal revenues in coined money rather than in kind, and set the pound-shilling-penny proportion that continued afterward among the English.

## modern-en · william-rufus · 20:5

In a country with neither foreign trade nor any of the finer manufactures, a great landowner has nothing to exchange for the bulk of his land's produce beyond what feeds the cultivators, so he consumes the whole surplus in lavish hospitality at home. Cited for Westminster Hall reportedly being his dining-room -- an anecdote on the retinue and hospitality of great households in early feudal England.

## modern-en · king-john-england · 19:9

The princes who were on the worst terms with their barons seem accordingly to have been the most generous in grants of this kind to their boroughs. King John of England, for example, appears to have been a most lavish benefactor to his towns. {See Madox.} Philip I of France lost all authority over his barons. Toward the end of his reign, his son Louis, later known as Louis the Fat, consulted (according to Father Daniel) the bishops of the royal demesnes about the best way to curb the violence of the great lords. Their advice came in two separate proposals. One was to create a new order of jurisdiction by establishing magistrates and a town council in every sizeable town of his demesnes. The other was to form a new militia by having the inhabitants of those towns, under the command of their own magistrates, march out when needed to the king's aid. It is from this period, according to French antiquaries, that we are to date the founding of the magistrates and councils of cities in France. It was during the unfortunate reigns of the princes of the house of Suabia that most of the free towns of Germany first received grants of their privileges, and that the famous Hanseatic league first became a formidable power. {See Pfeffel.}

"King John of England," cited as the most liberal English king in granting charters of privilege to his boroughs.

## modern-en · edward-i · 4:9

The names of these coins seem originally to have expressed the weight or quantity of metal they contained. In the time of Servius Tullius, who first coined money at Rome, the Roman as, or pondo, contained a Roman pound of good copper. It was divided, like our Troy pound, into twelve ounces, each of which contained a real ounce of good copper. The English pound sterling, in the time of Edward I, contained a pound, Tower weight, of silver of a known fineness. The Tower pound seems to have been somewhat more than the Roman pound and somewhat less than the Troy pound. The Troy pound was not introduced into the mint of England until the eighteenth year of Henry VIII. The French livre, in the time of Charlemagne, contained a pound, Troy weight, of silver of a known fineness. The fair of Troyes in Champagne was at that time attended by all the nations of Europe, and the weights and measures of so famous a market were generally known and respected. The Scots money pound, from the time of Alexander I to that of Robert Bruce, contained a pound of silver of the same weight and fineness as the English pound sterling. English, French, and Scots pennies, too, all originally contained a real pennyweight of silver—the twentieth part of an ounce, and the two-hundred-and-fortieth part of a pound. The shilling, too, seems originally to have been the name of a weight. “When wheat is at twelve shillings the quarter,” says an ancient statute of Henry III, “then the wastel bread of a farthing shall weigh eleven shillings and fourpence.” The proportion, however, between the shilling and either the penny on the one hand or the pound on the other does not seem to have been as constant and uniform as the proportion between the penny and the pound. During the first dynasty of the kings of France, the French sou, or shilling, appears on different occasions to have contained five, twelve, twenty, and forty pennies. Among the ancient Saxons a shilling appears at one time to have contained only five pennies, and it is not unlikely that it was as variable among them as among their neighbours, the ancient Franks. From the time of Charlemagne among the French, and from that of William the Conqueror among the English, the proportion between the pound, the shilling, and the penny seems to have been uniformly the same as it is now, though the value of each has changed greatly; for in every country in the world, I believe, the greed and injustice of princes and sovereign states, abusing the trust of their subjects, have gradually reduced the real quantity of metal originally contained in their coins. In the later ages of the republic, the Roman as was reduced to the twenty-fourth part of its original value, and instead of weighing a pound came to weigh only half an ounce. The English pound and penny at present contain only about a third, the Scots pound and penny about a thirty-sixth, and the French pound and penny about a sixty-sixth part of their original value. By means of these operations, the princes and sovereign states that carried them out were able, in appearance, to pay their debts and meet their obligations with a smaller quantity of silver than would otherwise have been required. It was, however, only in appearance, for their creditors were really cheated out of part of what was owed to them. All other debtors in the state were allowed the same privilege and could repay, with the same nominal sum in the new and debased coin, whatever they had borrowed in the old. Such operations, therefore, have always proved favourable to the debtor and ruinous to the creditor, and have sometimes produced a greater and more widespread revolution in the fortunes of private persons than could have been caused by a very great public disaster.

Cited for the weight and fineness of the pound sterling in his time, and for the Tower pound's introduction.

## modern-en · edward-iii · 5:25

The northern nations that established themselves on the ruins of the Roman empire seem to have had silver money from the very beginning of their settlements, and not to have known either gold or copper coins for several ages afterwards. There were silver coins in England in the time of the Saxons; but little gold was coined until the time of Edward III, nor any copper until that of James I of Great Britain. In England, therefore—and for the same reason, I believe, in all the other modern nations of Europe—all accounts are kept, and the value of all goods and of all estates is generally computed, in silver; and when we mean to express the size of a person's fortune, we seldom mention the number of guineas, but the number of pounds sterling we suppose would be given for it.

Cited for the point at which gold began to be coined in England, and for a Statute of Labourers regulating clerical and mason's pay.

## modern-en · edward-vi · 9:5

Under the 37th statute of Henry VIII, any interest above ten percent was declared illegal. Before that, it seems, more had sometimes been charged. In the reign of Edward VI, religious zeal banned interest altogether. But that ban, like every other of its kind, is said to have had no effect, and probably made the evil of usury worse rather than better. Henry VIII's statute was revived by the 13th statute of Elizabeth, chapter 8, and ten percent stayed the legal rate until the 21st statute of James I cut it to eight percent. Soon after the Restoration it dropped to six percent, and the 12th statute of Queen Anne brought it to five. All these statutory adjustments seem to have been very sensibly made. They appear to have followed the market rate rather than led it, matching the rate at which people of good credit usually borrowed. Since Queen Anne's time, five percent has been somewhat above the market rate rather than below it. Before the recent war, the government borrowed at three percent, and people of good credit in the capital and in many other parts of the kingdom borrowed at three and a half, four, and four and a half percent.

In whose reign "religious zeal prohibited all interest" -- a prohibition Smith says only increased the evil of usury.

English, French, and Scots pennies, too, all originally contained a real pennyweight of silver—the twentieth part of an ounce, and the two-hundred-and-fortieth part of a pound. The shilling, too, seems originally to have been the name of a weight. “When wheat is at twelve shillings the quarter,” says an ancient statute of Henry III, “then the wastel bread of a farthing shall weigh eleven shillings and fourpence.” The proportion, however, between the shilling and either the penny on the one hand or the pound on the other does not seem to have been as constant and uniform as the proportion between the penny and the pound. During the first dynasty of the kings of France, the French sou, or shilling, appears on different occasions to have contained five, twelve, twenty, and forty pennies. Among the ancient Saxons a shilling appears at one time to have contained only five pennies, and it is not unlikely that it was as variable among them as among their neighbours, the ancient Franks. From the time of Charlemagne among the French, and from that of William the Conqueror among the English, the proportion between the pound, the shilling, and the penny seems to have been uniformly the same as it is now, though the value of each has changed greatly; for in every country in the world, I believe, the greed and injustice of princes and sovereign states, abusing the trust of their subjects, have gradually reduced the real quantity of metal originally contained in their coins. In the later ages of the republic, the Roman as was reduced to the twenty-fourth part of its original value, and instead of weighing a pound came to weigh only half an ounce. The English pound and penny at present contain only about a third, the Scots pound and penny about a thirty-sixth, and the French pound and penny about a sixty-sixth part of their original value. By means of these operations, the princes and sovereign states that carried them out were able, in appearance, to pay their debts and meet their obligations with a smaller quantity of silver than would otherwise have been required. It was, however, only in appearance, for their creditors were really cheated out of part of what was owed to them. All other debtors in the state were allowed the same privilege and could repay, with the same nominal sum in the new and debased coin, whatever they had borrowed in the old. Such operations, therefore, have always proved favourable to the debtor and ruinous to the creditor, and have sometimes produced a greater and more widespread revolution in the fortunes of private persons than could have been caused by a very great public disaster.

Cited for introducing the Troyes pound weight into the English mint, and for the statute capping legal interest at ten per cent, later revived under Elizabeth.

## modern-en · philip-and-mary · 5:13

Rents that have been reserved in corn have kept their value much better than those reserved in money, even where the denomination of the coin has not been altered. By the 18th of Elizabeth it was enacted that a third of the rent of all college leases should be reserved in corn, to be paid either in kind or according to the current prices at the nearest public market. The money arising from this corn rent, though originally only a third of the whole, is, in the present day, according to Dr Blackstone, commonly close to double what arises from the other two-thirds. The old money rents of colleges must, by this account, have sunk to almost a fourth of their ancient value, or are worth little more than a fourth of the corn they were formerly worth. But since the reign of Philip and Mary the denomination of the English coin has undergone little or no alteration, and the same number of pounds, shillings, and pence has contained very nearly the same quantity of pure silver. This decline, therefore, in the value of the money rents of colleges has come entirely from the decline in the price of silver.

The joint English monarchs in whose reign the denomination of the English coin last underwent little alteration, and under whom the exportation of wheat was permitted with duty.

## modern-en · elizabeth-i · 5:13

Rents that have been reserved in corn have kept their value much better than those reserved in money, even where the denomination of the coin has not been altered. By the 18th of Elizabeth it was enacted that a third of the rent of all college leases should be reserved in corn, to be paid either in kind or according to the current prices at the nearest public market. Cited repeatedly for statutes: reserving college-lease rents partly in corn, reviving Henry VIII's interest cap, and the Statute of Apprenticeship requiring a seven-year term.

## modern-en · james-i · 5:25

The northern nations that established themselves on the ruins of the Roman empire seem to have had silver money from the very beginning of their settlements, and not to have known either gold or copper coins for several ages afterwards. There were silver coins in England in the time of the Saxons; but little gold was coined until the time of Edward III, nor any copper until that of James I of Great Britain. In England, therefore—and for the same reason, I believe, in all the other modern nations of Europe—all accounts are kept, and the value of all goods and of all estates is generally computed, in silver; and when we mean to express the size of a person's fortune, we seldom mention the number of guineas, but the number of pounds sterling we suppose would be given for it.

Cited for the introduction of copper coinage in Great Britain, and for restricting legal interest to eight per cent.

## modern-en · charles-i · 11:152

In 1688 Parliament granted the bounty on the export of corn. The country gentlemen, who then made up an even larger share of the legislature than they do now, had noticed that the money price of corn was falling. The bounty was a device meant to push it back up artificially to the high level at which it had often sold in the reigns of Charles I and II. It was therefore to remain in force until wheat reached forty-eight shillings a quarter—twenty shillings, or five-sevenths, dearer than the grower's price that King had estimated in that very year for times of moderate plenty. If his calculations deserve any of the wide reputation they have earned, forty-eight shillings a quarter was a price that, without some such device as the bounty, could not have been expected at that time except in years of extraordinary scarcity. But King William's government was not yet firmly established. It was in no position to refuse anything to the country gentlemen, from whom it was at that very moment seeking the first grant of the annual land-tax.

Named with Charles II for the prices at which wheat was frequently sold in their reigns.

## modern-en · charles-ii · 8:32

Over the course of the last century, taking one year with another, grain was dearer in both parts of the United Kingdom than during the present one. This is a fact that can no longer be reasonably doubted, and the proof is, if anything, even more decisive for Scotland than for England. In Scotland it rests on the public fiars — annual valuations, made on oath according to the actual state of the markets, of every sort of grain in every county of Scotland. If such direct proof needed any backing, I would add that the same has held in France, and probably in most of Europe. For France the proof is perfectly clear. But while it is certain that in both parts of the United Kingdom grain was somewhat dearer in the last century than now, it is equally certain that labour was much cheaper. So if the labouring poor could raise their families then, they must be much more comfortable now. In the last century the most usual day-wages for common labour across most of Scotland were sixpence in summer and fivepence in winter. Three shillings a week — very nearly the same rate — is still paid in parts of the Highlands and Western Isles. Across most of the Lowlands the most usual wage for common labour is now eightpence a day; tenpence, sometimes a shilling, around Edinburgh, in the counties bordering England — probably because of that nearness — and in a few other places where the demand for labour has lately risen considerably, around Glasgow, Carron, Ayrshire, and so on. In England, improvements in farming, manufacturing, and trade began much earlier than in Scotland. The demand for labour, and hence its price, must have risen with them. So in the last century as in the present, wages were higher in England than in Scotland. They have also risen considerably since then, though, because of the greater variety of rates paid in different places, it is harder to say by how much. In 1614 a foot soldier's pay was the same as today, eightpence a day. When the rate was first set, it would naturally have been pegged to the usual wage of common labourers, the class from which foot soldiers are usually drawn. Lord Chief Justice Hales, writing in the reign of Charles II, reckons the necessary cost of a labourer's family of six — father, mother, two children able to do something, and two who cannot — at ten shillings a week, or twenty-six pounds a year. If they cannot earn this by their labour, he supposes, they must make it up by begging or stealing. He seems to have looked into the matter very carefully. (See his scheme for the maintenance of the poor in Burn's History of the Poor Laws.) In 1688 Mr Gregory King, whose skill in political arithmetic Dr Davenant praises so highly, reckoned the ordinary income of labourers and out-servants at fifteen pounds a year per family, which he took to consist on average of three and a half persons. So his figure, though it looks different, comes very nearly to the same thing at bottom as Judge Hales's. Both put the weekly cost of such families at about twentypence a head. Both the cash income and the cash expense of such families have risen considerably since then through most of the kingdom — more in some places, less in others — though perhaps almost nowhere as much as some exaggerated accounts of present wages have lately led the public to believe. It must be noted that the price of labour can nowhere be pinned down very precisely, since different rates are often paid in the same place for the same kind of work, not only according to the worker's differing skill but according to the easiness or harshness of the employer. Where wages are not fixed by law, all we can claim to determine is what the most usual rates are; and experience seems to show that the law can never set them properly, however often it has pretended to.

Cited for Judge Hale's computation of labourers' family expense written in his time, for the statute fixing forty days' residence as a settlement, and for reducing legal interest to six per cent after the Restoration.

## modern-en · james-ii · 10:104

Some frauds, it is said, followed from this statute, parish officers sometimes bribing their own poor to slip off quietly to another parish and, by keeping out of sight for forty days, gain a settlement there, relieving the parish they really belonged to. So it was enacted, by the first year of James II, that the forty days of undisturbed residence needed to gain a settlement should be counted only from the time a person gave written notice, to one of the churchwardens or overseers of the parish he came to live in, of his address and the size of his family.

Cited for the statute clarifying that the forty days' residence needed for a parish settlement should run only from written notice.

## modern-en · william-iii · 5:36

On the reform of the silver coin in the reign of William III, the price of silver bullion still continued to be somewhat above the mint price. Mr Locke attributed this high price to the permission to export silver bullion and the prohibition against exporting silver coin. This permission to export, he said, made the demand for silver bullion greater than the demand for silver coin. But the number of people who want silver coin for the common purposes of buying and selling at home is surely much greater than the number who want silver bullion either for export or for any other use. There exists at present a similar permission to export gold bullion, and a similar prohibition against exporting gold coin; and yet the price of gold bullion has fallen below the mint price. But in the English coin, silver was then, just as now, under-rated in proportion to gold; and the gold coin (which at that time too was not supposed to need any reform) governed then, as well as now, the real value of the whole coinage. Since the reform of the silver coin did not then reduce the price of silver bullion to the mint price, it is not very likely that a similar reform would do so now.

Cited for the reformation of the silver coin in his reign, and for statutes on parish settlements.

## modern-en · queen-anne · 9:5

Under the 37th statute of Henry VIII, any interest above ten percent was declared illegal. Before that, it seems, more had sometimes been charged. In the reign of Edward VI, religious zeal banned interest altogether. But that ban, like every other of its kind, is said to have had no effect, and probably made the evil of usury worse rather than better. Henry VIII's statute was revived by the 13th statute of Elizabeth, chapter 8, and ten percent stayed the legal rate until the 21st statute of James I cut it to eight percent. Soon after the Restoration it dropped to six percent, and the 12th statute of Queen Anne brought it to five. All these statutory adjustments seem to have been very sensibly made. They appear to have followed the market rate rather than led it, matching the rate at which people of good credit usually borrowed. Since Queen Anne's time, five percent has been somewhat above the market rate rather than below it. Before the recent war, the government borrowed at three percent, and people of good credit in the capital and in many other parts of the kingdom borrowed at three and a half, four, and four and a half percent.

Cited for reducing legal interest to five per cent, and for statutes empowering bishops to set curates' stipends and clarifying settlement law.

## modern-en · george-ii · 10:118

In ancient times, too, it was usual to try to regulate the profits of merchants and other dealers by regulating the price of provisions and other goods. The assize of bread is, as far as I know, the only remnant of this ancient practice. Where there is an exclusive corporation, it may perhaps be proper to regulate the price of the first necessary of life; but where there is none, competition will regulate it far better than any assize. The method of fixing the assize of bread laid down by the thirty-first of George II could not be put into practice in Scotland, because of a defect in the law, its enforcement depending on the office of clerk of the market, which does not exist there. This defect was not remedied until the third of George III. The lack of an assize caused no noticeable inconvenience; and setting one up in the few places where it has so far been done has produced no noticeable benefit. In most of the towns of Scotland, however, there is an incorporation of bakers who claim exclusive privileges, though these are not very strictly guarded. The proportion between the different rates of wages and of profit in the different employments of labour and stock seems not to be much affected, as already noted, by the wealth or poverty, or the advancing, stationary, or declining state, of the society. Such upheavals in the public condition, though they affect the general rates both of wages and of profit, must in the end affect them equally across all the different employments. So the proportion between them must stay the same, and cannot well be altered, at least for any length of time, by any such upheavals.

Cited for the statute establishing the assize of bread, later found impracticable in Scotland for want of the office it depended on.

## modern-en · george-iii · 10:117

Particular acts of parliament, however, still sometimes try to regulate wages in particular trades and particular places. The eighth year of George III, for instance, forbids, under heavy penalties, all master tailors in London and within five miles of it from giving, and their workmen from accepting, more than two shillings and sevenpence halfpenny a day, except in a time of general mourning. Whenever the legislature tries to settle disputes between masters and their workmen, its advisers are always the masters. So when the regulation favours the workmen, it is always fair and equitable; but it is sometimes otherwise when it favours the masters. Thus the law that obliges the masters in several different trades to pay their workmen in money, not in goods, is quite fair and equitable. It imposes no real hardship on the masters. It only obliges them to pay in money the value they pretended to pay, but did not always really pay, in goods. That law favours the workmen; but the eighth of George III favours the masters. When masters combine to cut their workmen's wages, they commonly enter into a private bond or agreement not to give more than a certain wage, under a certain penalty. If the workmen were to enter into an opposite combination of the same kind, not to accept less than a certain wage, under a certain penalty, the law would punish them very severely; and if it dealt fairly, it would treat the masters the same way. But the eighth of George III enforces by law the very regulation that masters sometimes try to set up by such combinations. The workmen's complaint, that it puts the ablest and most industrious on the same footing as an ordinary workman, seems perfectly well founded.

Cited for the statute regulating London tailors' wages, and for the later remedy of the assize-of-bread defect in Scotland.

## modern-en · richard-ii · 31:177

The old customs were divided into three branches. The first, and perhaps the oldest of all these duties, was on wool and leather. It seems to have been chiefly or wholly an export duty. When the woollen manufacture became established in England, a like duty was laid on woollen cloths, so that the king should not lose any of his customs on wool through the export of cloth. The other two branches were, first, a duty on wine, which, being charged at so much a ton, was called a tonnage; and second, a duty on all other goods, which, being charged at so much per pound of their supposed value, was called a poundage. In the forty-seventh year of Edward III a duty of sixpence in the pound was laid on all goods exported and imported, except wool, woolfells, leather, and wine, which were subject to special duties. In the fourteenth year of Richard II this duty was raised to a shilling in the pound, but three years later it was reduced again to sixpence. It was raised to eightpence in the second year of Henry IV, and in the fourth year of the same king to a shilling. From then until the ninth year of William III this duty stayed at a shilling in the pound. The duties of tonnage and poundage were generally granted to the king by one and the same act of parliament, and were called the subsidy of tonnage and poundage. Because the subsidy of poundage stayed so long at a shilling in the pound, or five per cent, the word "subsidy" came in customs language to mean a general duty of this kind of five per cent. This subsidy, now called the old subsidy, is still levied according to the book of rates set by the twelfth year of Charles II. The method of fixing, by a book of rates, the value of goods subject to this duty is said to be older than the time of James I. The new subsidy, imposed in the ninth and tenth years of William III, was an additional five per cent on most goods. The one-third and two-third subsidies together made up another five per cent, of which they were proportionate parts. The subsidy of 1747 made a fourth five per cent on most goods, and that of 1759 a fifth on certain particular sorts of goods. Besides these five subsidies, a great variety of other duties have from time to time been laid on particular sorts of goods, sometimes to meet the state's emergencies and sometimes to regulate the country's trade according to the principles of the mercantile system.

Cited for a fourteenth-century statute raising a particular duty to one shilling in the pound.

## modern-en · robert-bruce · 4:9

The names of these coins seem originally to have expressed the weight or quantity of metal they contained. In the time of Servius Tullius, who first coined money at Rome, the Roman as, or pondo, contained a Roman pound of good copper. It was divided, like our Troy pound, into twelve ounces, each of which contained a real ounce of good copper. The English pound sterling, in the time of Edward I, contained a pound, Tower weight, of silver of a known fineness. The Tower pound seems to have been somewhat more than the Roman pound and somewhat less than the Troy pound. The Troy pound was not introduced into the mint of England until the eighteenth year of Henry VIII. The French livre, in the time of Charlemagne, contained a pound, Troy weight, of silver of a known fineness. The fair of Troyes in Champagne was at that time attended by all the nations of Europe, and the weights and measures of so famous a market were generally known and respected. The Scots money pound, from the time of Alexander I to that of Robert Bruce, contained a pound of silver of the same weight and fineness as the English pound sterling. English, French, and Scots pennies, too, all originally contained a real pennyweight of silver—the twentieth part of an ounce, and the two-hundred-and-fortieth part of a pound. The shilling, too, seems originally to have been the name of a weight. “When wheat is at twelve shillings the quarter,” says an ancient statute of Henry III, “then the wastel bread of a farthing shall weigh eleven shillings and fourpence.” The proportion, however, between the shilling and either the penny on the one hand or the pound on the other does not seem to have been as constant and uniform as the proportion between the penny and the pound. During the first dynasty of the kings of France, the French sou, or shilling, appears on different occasions to have contained five, twelve, twenty, and forty pennies. Among the ancient Saxons a shilling appears at one time to have contained only five pennies, and it is not unlikely that it was as variable among them as among their neighbours, the ancient Franks. From the time of Charlemagne among the French, and from that of William the Conqueror among the English, the proportion between the pound, the shilling, and the penny seems to have been uniformly the same as it is now, though the value of each has changed greatly; for in every country in the world, I believe, the greed and injustice of princes and sovereign states, abusing the trust of their subjects, have gradually reduced the real quantity of metal originally contained in their coins. In the later ages of the republic, the Roman as was reduced to the twenty-fourth part of its original value, and instead of weighing a pound came to weigh only half an ounce. The English pound and penny at present contain only about a third, the Scots pound and penny about a thirty-sixth, and the French pound and penny about a sixty-sixth part of their original value. By means of these operations, the princes and sovereign states that carried them out were able, in appearance, to pay their debts and meet their obligations with a smaller quantity of silver than would otherwise have been required. It was, however, only in appearance, for their creditors were really cheated out of part of what was owed to them. All other debtors in the state were allowed the same privilege and could repay, with the same nominal sum in the new and debased coin, whatever they had borrowed in the old. Such operations, therefore, have always proved favourable to the debtor and ruinous to the creditor, and have sometimes produced a greater and more widespread revolution in the fortunes of private persons than could have been caused by a very great public disaster.

Cited as the endpoint of the period during which the Scots money pound held the same weight and fineness of silver as the English pound sterling.

## modern-en · chief-baron-gilbert · 18:13

Yet it could never be in the interest even of this last kind of cultivator to put any part of the small savings he might set aside from his own share of the produce into further improving the land, because the landlord, who put in nothing, was to receive half of whatever it produced. The tithe, which is only a tenth of the produce, is found to be a very great obstacle to improvement. A charge amounting to one half, then, must have been an effective barrier to it. A metayer might have an interest in making the land yield as much as the proprietor's capital could draw out of it, but he could never have an interest in mixing any of his own capital into it. In France, where five-sixths of the whole kingdom is said to be still occupied by this kind of cultivator, the proprietors complain that their metayers seize every chance to use their master's cattle for carting rather than farming, because in carting they keep all the profit themselves, whereas in farming they share it with the landlord. This kind of tenant still exists in some parts of Scotland, where they are called steelbow tenants. The old English tenants whom Chief-Baron Gilbert and Dr Blackstone describe as having been bailiffs of the landlord rather than farmers in the proper sense were probably of the same kind.

Cited, with Dr. Blackstone, on the ancient status of English tenants as bailiffs rather than true tenants of their landlords.

## modern-en · doctor-burn · 10:106

"After all," says Doctor Burn, "this kind of settlement, by continuing forty days after publication of notice in writing, is very seldom obtained; and the design of the acts is not so much for gaining of settlements, as for the avoiding of them by persons coming into a parish clandestinely, for the giving of notice is only putting a force upon the parish to remove. But if a person's situation is such, that it is doubtful whether he is actually removable or not, he shall, by giving of notice, compel the parish either to allow him a settlement uncontested, by suffering him to continue forty days, or by removing him to try the right."

The historian of the poor laws, quoted repeatedly and at length on the law of parish settlements and certificates.

The English pound and penny at present contain only about a third, the Scots pound and penny about a thirty-sixth, and the French pound and penny about a sixty-sixth part of their original value. By means of these operations, the princes and sovereign states that carried them out were able, in appearance, to pay their debts and meet their obligations with a smaller quantity of silver than would otherwise have been required. It was, however, only in appearance, for their creditors were really cheated out of part of what was owed to them. All other debtors in the state were allowed the same privilege and could repay, with the same nominal sum in the new and debased coin, whatever they had borrowed in the old. Such operations, therefore, have always proved favourable to the debtor and ruinous to the creditor, and have sometimes produced a greater and more widespread revolution in the fortunes of private persons than could have been caused by a very great public disaster.

Cited for the French livre's original weight standard in his time, set by the Troyes weight then used at the fair of Troyes in Champagne.

## modern-en · king-john-france · 32:68

An augmentation, or direct raising of the coin's denomination, always is, and by its nature must be, an open and avowed operation. By it, pieces of smaller weight and bulk are called by the same name previously given to pieces of greater weight and bulk. Debasing the standard, by contrast, has generally been a concealed operation. By it, pieces are issued from the mint of the same denomination and, as nearly as could be arranged, of the same weight, bulk, and appearance as pieces already in circulation of much greater value. When King John of France, {See Du Cange, Glossary, under Moneta; the Benedictine edition.} debased his coin in order to pay his debts, all the officers of his mint were sworn to secrecy. Both operations are unjust. But a simple augmentation is an injustice of open violence, whereas a debasement is an injustice of treacherous fraud. So this latter operation, once discovered—and it could never be hidden very long—has always stirred up far greater indignation than the former. After any considerable augmentation, the coin has very seldom been brought back to its former weight; but after the greatest debasements, it has almost always been brought back to its former fineness. Hardly ever has anything else been able to appease the fury and indignation of the people.

"King John of France," cited (via Du Cange's Glossary) for adulterating his coin to pay his debts.

## modern-en · louis-vi · 19:9

The princes who were on the worst terms with their barons seem accordingly to have been the most generous in grants of this kind to their boroughs. King John of England, for example, appears to have been a most lavish benefactor to his towns. {See Madox.} Philip I of France lost all authority over his barons. Toward the end of his reign, his son Louis, later known as Louis the Fat, consulted (according to Father Daniel) the bishops of the royal demesnes about the best way to curb the violence of the great lords. Their advice came in two separate proposals. One was to create a new order of jurisdiction by establishing magistrates and a town council in every sizeable town of his demesnes. The other was to form a new militia by having the inhabitants of those towns, under the command of their own magistrates, march out when needed to the king's aid. It is from this period, according to French antiquaries, that we are to date the founding of the magistrates and councils of cities in France. It was during the unfortunate reigns of the princes of the house of Suabia that most of the free towns of Germany first received grants of their privileges, and that the famous Hanseatic league first became a formidable power. {See Pfeffel.}

Son of Philip I of France, known afterward as Louis the Fat (printed "Lewis the Fat"), who consulted the bishops of the royal demesnes, per Father Daniel, on restraining noble violence.

## modern-en · louis-xiv · 29:3

Mr Colbert, the famous minister of Louis XIV, was a man of integrity, great diligence, and command of detail. He had wide experience and a sharp eye for examining public accounts, and in short he had every ability needed to bring method and good order into the collection and spending of public revenue. Unfortunately, that minister had taken on all the prejudices of the mercantile system, which is by its very nature a system of restraint and regulation. Such a system could hardly fail to appeal to a hard-working, plodding man of business, used to managing the various departments of public offices and setting up the checks and controls needed to keep each one within its proper sphere. He tried to regulate the industry and commerce of a great country on the same model as the departments of a public office. Instead of letting every man pursue his own interest in his own way, on the liberal plan of equality, liberty, and justice, he granted special privileges to certain branches of industry while loading others with equally special restraints. Like other European ministers, he was inclined to favour the industry of the towns over that of the countryside; but he went further, and to prop up the towns' industry he was willing even to suppress and hold down that of the country. To make provisions cheap for townspeople, and so encourage manufactures and foreign trade, he banned the export of corn altogether, and thereby shut country people out of every foreign market for by far the most important part of what they produced. This ban, combined with the old provincial laws of France that restricted moving corn from one province to another, and with the arbitrary and degrading taxes levied on cultivators in almost every province, discouraged and held down French agriculture far below the level it would naturally have reached in such fertile soil and such a favourable climate. This state of discouragement and depression was felt to some degree in every part of the country, and many separate inquiries were launched into its causes. One of those causes appeared to be the preference that Mr Colbert's institutions gave to the industry of the towns over that of the country.

"Lewis XIV," whose famous minister Colbert is described as a man of probity, industry, and knowledge of detail, though mistaken in overvaluing town industry relative to the country.

## modern-en · philip-i-france · 19:9

The princes who were on the worst terms with their barons seem accordingly to have been the most generous in grants of this kind to their boroughs. King John of England, for example, appears to have been a most lavish benefactor to his towns. {See Madox.} Philip I of France lost all authority over his barons. Lost all effective authority over his barons; father of Louis the Fat.

## modern-en · robert-ii-france · 30:225

Since the establishment of the Pragmatic Sanction and the Concordat, the clergy of France have generally shown less respect for the decrees of the papal court than the clergy of any other Catholic country. In all the disputes their sovereign has had with the pope, they have almost always sided with the sovereign. This independence of the French clergy from the court of Rome seems to rest chiefly on the Pragmatic Sanction and the Concordat. In the earlier periods of the monarchy, the French clergy appear to have been as devoted to the pope as those of any other country. When Robert, the second prince of the Capetian line, was most unjustly excommunicated by the court of Rome, his own servants, it is said, threw the food that came from his table to the dogs, and refused to taste anything themselves that had been polluted by the touch of a man in his situation. They were taught to do this, we may safely assume, by the clergy of his own dominions.

"Robert, the second prince of the Capetian race," unjustly excommunicated by the court of Rome, whose own servants' reaction illustrates the era's clerical devotion.

## modern-en · colbert · 22:38

The case in which it may sometimes be a matter for deliberation how far it is proper to keep up the free importation of certain foreign goods is when some foreign nation restrains, by high duties or bans, the importation of some of our manufactures into its country. Revenge, in this case, naturally calls for retaliation, prompting us to impose the same duties and bans on the importation of some or all of their manufactures into ours. Nations, accordingly, seldom fail to retaliate this way. The French have been particularly quick to favour their own manufactures by restraining the importation of foreign goods that could compete with them. Much of Mr Colbert's policy consisted in this; despite his great abilities he seems in this case to have been taken in by the sophistry of merchants and manufacturers, who are always demanding a monopoly against their countrymen. It is now the opinion of the most intelligent men in France that his measures of this kind have not benefited his country. By the tariff of 1667 that minister imposed very high duties on a great many foreign manufactures. When he refused to ease them in favour of the Dutch, the Dutch in 1671 banned the importation of French wines, brandies, and manufactures. The war of 1672 seems to have been partly caused by this commercial dispute. The Peace of Nijmegen ended it in 1678 by easing some of those duties in favour of the Dutch, who in turn lifted their ban. It was about the same time that the French and English began mutually to burden each other's industry with the same kind of duties and bans—of which the French, however, seem to have set the first example. The hostility between the two nations ever since has so far prevented any easing on either side. In 1697 the English banned the importation of bone lace, a Flanders manufacture. The government of that country, then under Spanish rule, banned in return the importation of English woollens. In 1700 the ban on importing bone lace into England was lifted, on condition that the importation of English woollens into Flanders be put back on its former footing.

Louis XIV's famous minister, a man of probity and great ability who nonetheless, per Smith, overvalued the industry of towns above that of the country in his economic policy.

## modern-en · choiseul · 30:217

In all Christian churches the clergy's benefices are a kind of freehold, which they hold not at someone's pleasure but for life or during good behaviour. If they held them on a more precarious footing, liable to be turned out at every slight annoyance to the sovereign or his ministers, it might be impossible for them to keep their authority with the people, who would then regard them as mercenary hangers-on of the court, in the sincerity of whose teaching they could no longer have any confidence. But should the sovereign try, in an irregular and violent way, to deprive any number of clergymen of their freeholds—perhaps because they had spread some factious or seditious doctrine with more than usual zeal—such persecution would only make both them and their doctrine ten times more popular, and so ten times more troublesome and dangerous, than they had been before. Fear is in almost every case a wretched instrument of government, and ought in particular never to be used against any order of men who have the slightest claim to independence. Trying to terrify them only irritates their bad temper and confirms them in an opposition that gentler treatment might perhaps easily induce them either to soften or to drop altogether. The violence the French government usually used to force all its parlements, or sovereign courts of justice, to register an unpopular edict very seldom succeeded. The means commonly used—imprisoning all the defiant members—one would think forcible enough. The princes of the house of Stuart sometimes used similar means to influence some members of the English Parliament, and generally found them just as stubborn. The English Parliament is now managed in another way; and a very small experiment that the Duke of Choiseul made about twelve years ago on the Parlement of Paris showed clearly enough that all the parlements of France might have been managed still more easily in the same way. That experiment was not pursued. For although management and persuasion are always the easiest and safest instruments of government, while force and violence are the worst and most dangerous, yet such, it seems, is man's natural insolence that he almost always disdains to use the good instrument except when he cannot or dare not use the bad one. The French government could and dared to use force, and therefore disdained to use management and persuasion. But there is no order of men, I believe, on whom it is so dangerous—so utterly ruinous—to use force and violence, as the experience of all ages shows, as the respected clergy of an established church. The rights, the privileges, the personal liberty of every individual clergyman who is on good terms with his own order are, even in the most despotic governments, more respected than those of any other person of nearly equal rank and fortune. This holds at every level of despotism, from the gentle and mild government of Paris to the violent and furious government of Constantinople. But though this order of men can scarcely ever be forced, they may be managed as easily as any other; and the security of the sovereign, as well as the public peace, seems to depend very much on his means of managing them—and those means seem to consist entirely in the preferment he has to bestow on them.

Cited for a small experiment made about twelve years before writing, upon the parliament of Paris.

## modern-en · quesnay · 29:38

Still, for all its flaws, this system is perhaps the closest approach to the truth yet published on the subject of political economy, and for that reason it deserves the attention of anyone who wants to study the principles of that very important science carefully. Its claim that only labour applied to land is productive is, admittedly, too narrow and cramped. But when it says that the wealth of nations lies not in the useless hoard of money but in the consumable goods that the society's labour reproduces each year, and when it says that perfect liberty is the only effective way to make that yearly reproduction as large as possible, its doctrine seems entirely as sound as it is generous and liberal. Its followers are very numerous. Because people love paradoxes and love seeming to grasp what is beyond ordinary minds, the paradox it defends—that manufacturing labour is unproductive—has probably done a fair bit to swell its ranks of admirers. For some years now they have formed a sizeable sect, known in the French literary world as the Economists. Their writings have certainly done their country some good, both by opening up many subjects to general discussion that had never been properly examined before, and by nudging public policy somewhat in favour of agriculture. It is thanks to their arguments that French agriculture has been freed from several of the burdens it once laboured under. The term for which a lease can be granted so as to hold good against any future buyer or owner of the land has been extended from nine years to twenty-seven. The old provincial barriers on moving grain from one province of the kingdom to another have been wholly removed, and the freedom to export grain to all foreign countries has been made the common law of the kingdom in all ordinary cases. This sect's writings are very numerous, and they deal not only with what is properly called political economy—the nature and causes of the wealth of nations—but with every other branch of civil government, and in all of them the authors follow the doctrine of Mr Quesnai faithfully and without any noticeable variation. Because of this, most of their works show little variety. The clearest and best-organized account of the doctrine is in a small book by Mr Mercier de la Riviere, formerly intendant of Martinico, called The Natural and Essential Order of Political Societies. The whole sect's admiration for their master—a man himself of the greatest modesty and simplicity—is no less than that of any of the ancient philosophers for the founders of their own systems. 'Since the world began,' says the diligent and respected Marquis de Mirabeau, 'there have been three great inventions that have chiefly given stability to political societies, apart from the many others that have enriched and adorned them. The first is the invention of writing, which alone gives human nature the power to pass on its laws, contracts, records, and discoveries without alteration. The second is the invention of money, which ties together all the relations among civilized societies. The third is the economical table, which grows out of the other two and completes them both by perfecting their purpose—the great discovery of our age, though it is our descendants who will reap its benefit.'

"The very ingenious and profound author" of the physiocratic economic system Smith examines and critiques at length; a physician by training.

## modern-en · voltaire · 30:237

In countries where most church benefices are very modest, a university chair is generally a better position than a church benefice. In that case the universities have the pick of all the churchmen of the country, who in every country make up by far the most numerous class of men of letters. Where many of the church benefices, on the contrary, are very considerable, the church naturally draws away from the universities most of their eminent men of letters, who generally find some patron who does himself credit by getting them church preferment. In the first case we are likely to find the universities filled with the most eminent men of letters in the country. In the second we are likely to find few eminent men among them, and those few among the youngest members of the body, who are likely, too, to be drained away from it before they can have gained enough experience and knowledge to be of much use to it. Voltaire notes that Father Porée, a Jesuit of no great standing in the republic of letters, was the only professor France ever had whose works were worth reading. In a country that has produced so many eminent men of letters, it must seem rather odd that scarcely one of them should have been a university professor. The famous Gassendi was, early in his life, a professor at the University of Aix. When his genius first began to show, it was pointed out to him that by going into the church he could easily find a much quieter and more comfortable living, as well as a better situation for pursuing his studies; and he at once took the advice. Voltaire's observation may, I believe, be applied not only to France but to all other Roman Catholic countries. We very rarely find in any of them an eminent man of letters who is a university professor, except perhaps in the professions of law and medicine, professions from which the church is not so likely to draw them away. After the Church of Rome, that of England is by far the richest and best-endowed church in Christendom. In England, accordingly, the church is continually draining the universities of all their best and ablest members; and an old college tutor known and distinguished across Europe as an eminent man of letters is as rarely to be found there as in any Roman Catholic country. In Geneva, on the contrary, in the Protestant cantons of Switzerland, in the Protestant countries of Germany, in Holland, in Scotland, in Sweden, and in Denmark, the most eminent men of letters those countries have produced have—not all, indeed, but the far greater part of them—been university professors. In those countries the universities are continually draining the church of all its most eminent men of letters.

Cited for the anecdote of father Porée, an unremarkable Jesuit professor whose students nonetheless all followed his literary advice.
