## modern-en · columbus · 27:8

Some years before this, while Europe was still in suspense about the Portuguese projects, whose success still looked uncertain, a Genoese navigator formed the even bolder plan of sailing to the East Indies by going west. The location of those countries was at that time very poorly known in Europe. The few European travellers who had been there had exaggerated the distance, perhaps out of simple ignorance, since what was genuinely vast seemed almost infinite to people who had no way to measure it, or perhaps to make their own adventures in visiting such remote regions seem even more marvellous. The longer the eastward route was, Columbus rightly concluded, the shorter the westward route would be. He therefore proposed to take the western route, as both the shortest and the surest, and he had the good luck to convince Isabella of Castile that his plan was likely to work. He sailed from the port of Palos in August 1492, nearly five years before Vasco da Gama set out from Portugal, and after a voyage of two to three months he discovered first some of the small Bahama or Lucayan islands, and then the great island of Saint Domingo.

Concluded that the shorter way to the East Indies lay west, convinced Isabella of Castile of his project, and, per his own letters to Ferdinand and Isabella, insisted the lands he found were the Indies even against clear evidence -- a mistake that gave the region its lasting name.

## modern-en · vasco-da-gama · 27:7

The great profits of the Venetians stirred the greed of the Portuguese. Throughout the fifteenth century they had been trying to find a sea route to the countries from which the Moors brought them ivory and gold dust across the desert. They discovered the Madeiras, the Canaries, the Azores, the Cape Verde islands, the coast of Guinea, and those of Loango, Congo, Angola, and Benguela, and finally the Cape of Good Hope. They had long wanted a share in the Venetians' profitable trade, and this last discovery opened up a real prospect of getting it. In 1497 Vasco da Gama sailed from Lisbon with a fleet of four ships, and after eleven months at sea reached the coast of Hindustan, completing a course of discoveries that had been pursued steadily, with very little interruption, for nearly a century.

Sailed from Lisbon in 1497 (printed "Vasco de Gamo"), nearly five years after Columbus's own expedition set out.

## modern-en · ferdinand-of-aragon · 27:9

But the countries Columbus discovered, on this voyage or any later one, bore no resemblance to the ones he had set out to find. Instead of the wealth, cultivation, and dense population of China and Hindustan, all he found in Saint Domingo and every other part of the new world he ever visited was a land entirely covered with forest, uncultivated, and inhabited only by some tribes of naked and miserable savages. Even so, he was reluctant to believe these were not some of the countries described by Marco Polo, the first European to visit China or the East Indies, or at least the first to leave any description of them; and a very slight resemblance, such as the one he found between Cibao, a mountain in Saint Domingo, and Cipango, mentioned by Marco Polo, was often enough to send him back to this favourite idea, even against the clearest evidence. In his letters to Ferdinand and Isabella he called the countries he had discovered the Indies. He had no doubt that they were the far edge of the lands Marco Polo had described, and that they lay not far from the Ganges or from the countries Alexander had conquered. Even when he was finally convinced they were different, he still flattered himself that those rich countries were close by, and on a later voyage he accordingly went looking for them along the coast of Terra Firma and towards the Isthmus of Darien.

Named with Isabella as the recipient of Columbus's letters insisting his discoveries were the Indies.

## modern-en · isabella-of-castile · 27:8

Some years before this, while Europe was still in suspense about the Portuguese projects, whose success still looked uncertain, a Genoese navigator formed the even bolder plan of sailing to the East Indies by going west. The location of those countries was at that time very poorly known in Europe. The few European travellers who had been there had exaggerated the distance, perhaps out of simple ignorance, since what was genuinely vast seemed almost infinite to people who had no way to measure it, or perhaps to make their own adventures in visiting such remote regions seem even more marvellous. The longer the eastward route was, Columbus rightly concluded, the shorter the westward route would be. He therefore proposed to take the western route, as both the shortest and the surest, and he had the good luck to convince Isabella of Castile that his plan was likely to work. He sailed from the port of Palos in August 1492, nearly five years before Vasco da Gama set out from Portugal, and after a voyage of two to three months he discovered first some of the small Bahama or Lucayan islands, and then the great island of Saint Domingo.

Convinced by Columbus of his project's probability, and, with Ferdinand, the recipient of his letters on the supposed Indies.

## modern-en · cortes · 27:18

All the other Spanish ventures in the New World after Columbus's seem to have been driven by the same motive. It was the sacred thirst for gold that carried Oviedo, Nicuesa, and Vasco Núñez de Balboa to the Isthmus of Darien; that carried Cortés to Mexico, and Almagro and Pizarro to Chile and Peru. Whenever these adventurers reached an unknown coast, their first question was always whether there was any gold to be found there; and according to what they learned about this, they decided either to leave the country or to settle in it.

## modern-en · montezuma · 27:31

From the moment its colonies were first established, the crown of Spain drew some revenue from them through its share of the gold and silver. It was also a revenue of a kind to excite in human greed the most extravagant expectation of still greater riches. So the Spanish colonies, from the moment they were first established, drew a great deal of attention from their mother country, while those of the other European nations were for a long time largely neglected. The former perhaps did not thrive any better because of this attention, nor the latter any worse because of this neglect. In proportion to the extent of the country they to some degree occupy, the Spanish colonies are reckoned less populous and thriving than those of almost any other European nation. Even so, the Spanish colonies' progress in population and improvement has certainly been very rapid and very great. The city of Lima, founded since the conquest, is described by Ulloa as containing fifty thousand inhabitants nearly thirty years ago. Quito, which had been only a miserable hamlet of Indians, is described by the same author as being equally populous in his own time. Gemelli Careri, a supposed traveller it is said, but who seems everywhere to have written from very good information, describes the city of Mexico as containing a hundred thousand inhabitants, a number which, despite all the exaggerations of the Spanish writers, is probably more than five times what it held in the time of Montezuma. These figures greatly exceed those of Boston, New York, and Philadelphia, the three largest cities of the English colonies. Before the Spanish conquest there were no draught animals fit for hauling in either Mexico or Peru. The lama was their only beast of burden, and its strength seems to have been a good deal less than that of a common ass. The plough was unknown to them. They had no knowledge of iron. They had no coined money, nor any established medium of trade of any kind. Their commerce was carried on by barter. A kind of wooden spade was their main farming tool. Sharp stones served them as knives and hatchets to cut with; fish bones and the hard sinews of certain animals served them as needles to sew with; and these seem to have been their main tools of trade. In such a state of things, it seems impossible that either of these empires could have been as improved or as well cultivated as it is now, when they are plentifully supplied with all kinds of European livestock, and when the use of iron, the plough, and many of the arts of Europe have been introduced among them. But the populousness of any country must be in proportion to the degree of its improvement and cultivation. Despite the cruel destruction of the natives that followed the conquest, these two great empires are probably more populous now than they ever were before; and the people are surely very different, for we must admit, I think, that the Spanish creoles are in many respects superior to the ancient Indians.

Cited for the population of Mexico City in his time, compared to its present state.

## modern-en · walter-raleigh · 27:20

But although the judgment of sober reason and experience about such ventures has always been extremely unfavourable, human greed has commonly judged quite otherwise. The same passion that has prompted so many people with the absurd idea of the philosopher's stone has prompted others with the equally absurd idea of immensely rich gold and silver mines. They failed to consider that the value of those metals has in all ages and nations come chiefly from their scarcity, that their scarcity comes from the very small quantities nature has deposited anywhere in one place, from the hard and stubborn materials with which she has almost everywhere surrounded those small quantities, and therefore from the labour and expense everywhere needed to break through to them and get at them. They imagined that veins of those metals might be found in many places, as large and abundant as the veins commonly found of lead, copper, tin, or iron. Sir Walter Raleigh's dream of the golden city and country of El Dorado may show us that even wise men are not always free of such strange delusions. More than a hundred years after that great man's death, the Jesuit Gumilla was still convinced that wonderful country was real, and expressed with great warmth, and I dare say great sincerity, how glad he would be to carry the light of the gospel to a people who could so well reward the pious labours of their missionary.

"The dream of Sir Waiter Raleigh" (a probable compositor misprint for Walter, corrected in the modern-en edition), concerning the golden city and country of El Dorado, and his patents to the London and Plymouth companies.

## modern-en · hobbes · 5:3

Wealth, as Mr Hobbes says, is power. But the person who either earns or inherits a great fortune does not necessarily gain or inherit any political power, whether civil or military. His fortune may perhaps give him the means of acquiring both; but merely owning that fortune does not automatically convey either to him. The power that owning it immediately and directly conveys to him is the power to purchase a certain command over all the labour, or over all the produce of labour, that is then in the market. His fortune is greater or smaller exactly in proportion to the extent of this power—that is, to the quantity of other men's labour, or, what amounts to the same thing, of the produce of other men's labour, which it enables him to buy or command. The exchangeable value of everything must always be exactly equal to the extent of the power it conveys to its owner.

Quoted directly: "Wealth, as Mr Hobbes says, is power" -- though Smith immediately qualifies that a fortune's mere possession does not itself convey political power.

## modern-en · locke · 5:36

On the reform of the silver coin in the reign of William III, the price of silver bullion still continued to be somewhat above the mint price. Mr Locke attributed this high price to the permission to export silver bullion and the prohibition against exporting silver coin. This permission to export, he said, made the demand for silver bullion greater than the demand for silver coin. But the number of people who want silver coin for the common purposes of buying and selling at home is surely much greater than the number who want silver bullion either for export or for any other use. There exists at present a similar permission to export gold bullion, and a similar prohibition against exporting gold coin; and yet the price of gold bullion has fallen below the mint price. But in the English coin, silver was then, just as now, under-rated in proportion to gold; and the gold coin (which at that time too was not supposed to need any reform) governed then, as well as now, the real value of the whole coinage. Since the reform of the silver coin did not then reduce the price of silver bullion to the mint price, it is not very likely that a similar reform would do so now.

Cited three times: on the high price of silver bullion after the reformation of the coin under William III, alongside Law and Montesquieu on money and interest, and on the distinction between money and other moveable goods.

## modern-en · hume · 11:218

In countries that are poorly cultivated, and therefore thinly inhabited, the price of the wool and the hide always bears a much greater proportion to that of the whole beast than in countries where, improvement and population being further advanced, there is more demand for butcher's meat. Mr Hume observes that in Saxon times the fleece was reckoned at two-fifths of the value of the whole sheep, and that this was much above the proportion of its present valuation. In some provinces of Spain, I have been assured, the sheep is often killed merely for the sake of the fleece and the tallow. The carcase is often left to rot on the ground or to be devoured by beasts and birds of prey. If this sometimes happens even in Spain, it happens almost constantly in Chile, at Buenos Ayres, and in many other parts of Spanish America, where the horned cattle are almost constantly killed merely for the sake of the hide and the tallow. This too used to happen almost constantly in Hispaniola, while it was infested by the buccaneers, and before the settlement, improvement, and populousness of the French plantations—which now extend round the coast of almost the whole western half of the island—had given some value to the cattle of the Spaniards, who still continue to hold not only the eastern part of the coast but the whole inland mountainous part of the country.

Cited for the observation that in Saxon times the fleece was valued at two-fifths of the whole sheep's worth.

## modern-en · montesquieu · 9:16

When the law bans interest altogether, it does not stop it. Many people have to borrow, and no one will lend without some payment for the use of their money, payment suited not only to what can be made by using it but to the difficulty and danger of evading the law. The high interest rates among all Muslim nations are explained by Montesquieu not by their poverty but partly by this and partly by the difficulty of recovering the money.

Named with Locke and Law on money and interest, and separately for accounting for high interest rates among Mahometan nations by legal prohibition rather than poverty.

## modern-en · john-law · 13:79

That Scotland's industry languished for want of money to employ it was the opinion of the famous Mr Law. By setting up a particular kind of bank, which he seems to have imagined could issue paper to the full value of all the land in the country, he proposed to cure this shortage of money. When he first proposed his scheme, the parliament of Scotland did not see fit to adopt it. It was later adopted, with some changes, by the Duke of Orleans, at that time regent of France. The notion that paper money could be multiplied to almost any extent was the real foundation of what is called the Mississippi scheme, the most extravagant project in both banking and stock-jobbing the world has perhaps ever seen. Mr Du Verney explains the various operations of this scheme so fully, so clearly, and with such order and precision, in his Examination of the Political Reflections on the commerce and finances of Mr Du Tot, that I shall give no account of them here. The principles it rested on are explained by Mr Law himself in a discourse on money and trade, which he published in Scotland when he first proposed his scheme. The splendid but visionary ideas set out in that and other works on the same principles still impress many people, and have perhaps contributed in part to the excess of banking lately complained of, both in Scotland and elsewhere.

"The famous Mr Law," who believed a bank could increase a country's productive capital and established one on that principle, explained in his own published discourse on money and trade.

## modern-en · josiah-child · 30:104

The terms of admission into the Hamburgh Company are now said to be quite easy, and the directors either have no power to subject the trade to any troublesome restraints or regulations, or at least have not lately used that power. It was not always so. About the middle of the last century the fine for admission was fifty pounds, and at one time a hundred, and the company's conduct was said to be extremely oppressive. In 1643, 1645, and 1661, the clothiers and free traders of the west of England complained of them to parliament as monopolists who restricted the trade and oppressed the country's manufactures. Although these complaints produced no act of parliament, they had probably so intimidated the company as to force it to reform its conduct; at least there have been no complaints against it since that time. By the 10th and 11th of William III, c. 6, the fine for admission into the Russia Company was reduced to five pounds, and by the 25th of Charles II, c. 7, that for admission into the Eastland Company to forty shillings, while at the same time Sweden, Denmark, and Norway, all the countries on the north side of the Baltic, were exempted from its exclusive charter. The conduct of these companies had probably given occasion to these two acts of parliament. Before that time Sir Josiah Child had described both these and the Hamburgh Company as extremely oppressive, and blamed their bad management for the low state of the trade we then carried on to the countries within their respective charters. But though such companies may not be very oppressive in the present day, they are certainly altogether useless. To be merely useless is perhaps the highest praise that can ever justly be given to a regulated company, and all three companies named above seem, in their present state, to deserve this praise.

Represented the Hamburgh and other regulated companies as harmful exclusive monopolies; a regulated company was still established long after his time, in 1750.

## modern-en · thomas-mun · 21:7

First, they argued that exporting gold and silver to buy foreign goods did not always reduce the quantity of those metals in the kingdom; on the contrary, it might often increase it. If the country's consumption of foreign goods was not raised as a result, those goods could be re-exported to other countries and sold there for a large profit, bringing back far more treasure than had originally been sent out to buy them. Mun compares this working of foreign trade to the sowing and harvest of farming. “If we only look,” he says, “at what the farmer does at sowing time, when he throws away much good corn into the ground, we will think him a madman rather than a farmer. But when we consider his work at harvest, the goal of all his effort, we find the value and the abundant increase in what he has done.”

Compared the operation of foreign trade to seed-time and harvest; his book, "England's Treasure in Foreign Trade," gave its title to a fundamental maxim of the mercantile system.

## modern-en · matthew-decker · 25:20

The bounty on corn exports works in exactly the same way as this absurd policy of Spain and Portugal. Whatever the actual state of farming, it makes our corn somewhat dearer in the home market than it would otherwise be, and somewhat cheaper in the foreign market; and since the average money price of corn governs, more or less, that of all other goods, it lowers the value of silver considerably in the home market and tends to raise it a little abroad. It lets foreigners—the Dutch in particular—not only eat our corn more cheaply than they otherwise could, but sometimes eat it more cheaply than even our own people can on the same occasions, as we are assured by an excellent authority, Sir Matthew Decker. It stops our own workers from supplying their goods for as little silver as they otherwise might, while letting the Dutch supply theirs for less. It tends to make our manufactures somewhat dearer in every market and theirs somewhat cheaper than they would otherwise be, and so gives their industry a double advantage over ours.

"An excellent authority," cited repeatedly on the effects of taxes on the price of goods, and for his well-known proposal that all commodities be taxed by an annual licence rather than at the point of sale.

## modern-en · cantillon · 8:14

A man has to live by his work, so his wages must at least be enough to keep him alive. In most cases they must be somewhat more, or he could never raise a family, and that kind of worker would die out after a single generation. On this reasoning Mr Cantillon seems to assume that the lowest sort of common labourer must everywhere earn at least double his own upkeep, so that on average he can raise two children — the wife's labour being taken as no more than enough to support herself, given that she must care for the children. But of the children born, it is reckoned, half die before reaching adulthood. So, by this account, the poorest labourers must on average try to raise at least four children, so that two have a fair chance of living that long. And the necessary upkeep of four children is supposed to come to roughly that of one grown man. The same author adds that the labour of an able-bodied slave is reckoned worth double his upkeep, and that the labour of the meanest worker, he thinks, cannot be worth less than that of an able-bodied slave. This much at least seems certain: to raise a family, the labour of husband and wife together, even in the lowest kind of common labour, must earn something more than is strictly needed for their own upkeep. But in what proportion — whether the one just mentioned, or some other — I will not undertake to decide.

Computed that the lowest species of common labourers must earn at least double their own maintenance in order to rear children to working age.

## modern-en · adam-anderson · 11:120

In an ancient manuscript of the Regiam Majestatem, an old Scottish law book, there is a statute of assize in which the price of bread is regulated according to all the different prices of wheat, from tenpence to three shillings the Scotch boll, equal to about half an English quarter. Three shillings Scotch, at the time this assize is supposed to have been passed, were equal to about nine shillings sterling of our present money. Mr Ruddiman seems (see his Preface to Anderson's Diplomata Scotiae) to conclude from this that three shillings was the highest price wheat ever reached in those days, and that tenpence, a shilling, or at most two shillings were the ordinary prices. But on consulting the manuscript it is plainly clear that all these prices are set down only as examples of the proportion to be kept between the respective prices of wheat and bread. The statute's last words are "reliqua judicabis secundum praescripta, habendo respectum ad pretium bladi": "You shall judge the remaining cases according to what is written above, having regard to the price of corn."

Compiler of the Diplomata Scotiae, to whose preface (by Ruddiman) Smith refers for the historical proportion of gold to silver in Scots coin.

## modern-en · james-postlethwaite · 13:81

In 1697 the bank was allowed to enlarge its capital stock by an addition of £1,001,171:10s., bringing its whole capital stock at that time to £2,201,171:10s. This addition is said to have been made to support public credit. In 1696, tallies had been at a forty, fifty, and sixty per cent. discount, and bank notes at twenty per cent. {James Postlethwaite's History of the Public Revenue, p.301.} During the great re-coinage of the silver then under way, the bank had seen fit to suspend payment of its notes, which inevitably brought them into discredit.

Author of the History of the Public Revenue, cited for figures on wartime exchequer discounts and on debt added during a war's continuance.

## modern-en · davenant · 8:32

Over the course of the last century, taking one year with another, grain was dearer in both parts of the United Kingdom than during the present one. This is a fact that can no longer be reasonably doubted, and the proof is, if anything, even more decisive for Scotland than for England. In Scotland it rests on the public fiars — annual valuations, made on oath according to the actual state of the markets, of every sort of grain in every county of Scotland. If such direct proof needed any backing, I would add that the same has held in France, and probably in most of Europe. For France the proof is perfectly clear. But while it is certain that in both parts of the United Kingdom grain was somewhat dearer in the last century than now, it is equally certain that labour was much cheaper. So if the labouring poor could raise their families then, they must be much more comfortable now. In the last century the most usual day-wages for common labour across most of Scotland were sixpence in summer and fivepence in winter. Three shillings a week — very nearly the same rate — is still paid in parts of the Highlands and Western Isles. Across most of the Lowlands the most usual wage for common labour is now eightpence a day; tenpence, sometimes a shilling, around Edinburgh, in the counties bordering England — probably because of that nearness — and in a few other places where the demand for labour has lately risen considerably, around Glasgow, Carron, Ayrshire, and so on. In England, improvements in farming, manufacturing, and trade began much earlier than in Scotland. The demand for labour, and hence its price, must have risen with them. So in the last century as in the present, wages were higher in England than in Scotland. They have also risen considerably since then, though, because of the greater variety of rates paid in different places, it is harder to say by how much. In 1614 a foot soldier's pay was the same as today, eightpence a day. When the rate was first set, it would naturally have been pegged to the usual wage of common labourers, the class from which foot soldiers are usually drawn. Lord Chief Justice Hales, writing in the reign of Charles II, reckons the necessary cost of a labourer's family of six — father, mother, two children able to do something, and two who cannot — at ten shillings a week, or twenty-six pounds a year. If they cannot earn this by their labour, he supposes, they must make it up by begging or stealing. He seems to have looked into the matter very carefully. (See his scheme for the maintenance of the poor in Burn's History of the Poor Laws.) In 1688 Mr Gregory King, whose skill in political arithmetic Dr Davenant praises so highly, reckoned the ordinary income of labourers and out-servants at fifteen pounds a year per family, which he took to consist on average of three and a half persons. So his figure, though it looks different, comes very nearly to the same thing at bottom as Judge Hales's. Both put the weekly cost of such families at about twentypence a head. Both the cash income and the cash expense of such families have risen considerably since then through most of the kingdom — more in some places, less in others — though perhaps almost nowhere as much as some exaggerated accounts of present wages have lately led the public to believe. It must be noted that the price of labour can nowhere be pinned down very precisely, since different rates are often paid in the same place for the same kind of work, not only according to the worker's differing skill but according to the easiness or harshness of the employer. Where wages are not fixed by law, all we can claim to determine is what the most usual rates are; and experience seems to show that the law can never set them properly, however often it has pretended to.

Extolled the political-arithmetic skill of Mr. Gregory King, whose computation of labourers' income Smith cites.

## modern-en · lowndes · 11:147

There was a third event in this same period which, though it could not cause any scarcity of corn, nor perhaps any rise in the real quantity of silver usually paid for it, must necessarily have caused some rise in the nominal sum. This event was the great debasement of the silver coin by clipping and wearing. This evil had begun in the reign of Charles II and had gone on steadily increasing until 1695, at which time, as we may learn from Mr Lowndes, the current silver coin was on average near twenty-five per cent below its standard value. But the nominal sum that makes up the market price of every commodity is necessarily governed not so much by the quantity of silver that, according to the standard, ought to be in it, as by the quantity that experience finds actually is in it. This nominal sum, then, is necessarily higher when the coin is much debased by clipping and wearing than when it is near its standard value.

Author of the Essay on the Silver Coin, cited for the market price of silver bullion around 1695.

## modern-en · dupre-de-st-maur · 11:111

Its price during the same period had probably sunk in the same way through most of Europe.

One of the "faithful, diligent, and laborious collectors of the prices of corn," cited repeatedly alongside Messance for French grain-price data.

## modern-en · messance · 8:47

A French writer of great knowledge and ingenuity, Mr Messance, receiver of the taille in the election district of Saint-Étienne, tries to show that the poor do more work in cheap years than in dear ones, by comparing the quantity and value of goods produced in those different conditions in three different industries: one of coarse woollens, carried on at Elbeuf, and one of linen and another of silk, both spread across the whole generality of Rouen. From his account, copied from the registers of the public offices, it appears that the quantity and value of goods made in all three industries has generally been greater in cheap years than in dear ones, and has always been greatest in the cheapest years and least in the dearest. All three seem to be stationary industries — ones whose output may vary somewhat from year to year but which, on the whole, are neither advancing nor declining.

"A French author of great knowledge and ingenuity," who showed that the poor produced more goods in cheap than in dear years across three French manufactures, and who collected French grain-price data with Dupré de St. Maur.

## modern-en · meggens · 11:174

According to Mr Meggens (Postscript to the Universal Merchant, pp. 15 and 16—this postscript was not printed until 1756, three years after the book itself, which has never had a second edition, so the postscript is found in few copies; it corrects several errors in the book), the annual import of precious metals into Spain, averaged over six years from 1748 to 1753 inclusive, and into Portugal, averaged over seven years from 1747 to 1753 inclusive, came to 1,101,107 pounds weight of silver and 49,940 pounds weight of gold. The silver, at sixty-two shillings the pound troy, amounts to £3,413,431:10s. sterling. The gold, at forty-four guineas and a half the pound troy, amounts to £2,333,446:14s. sterling. Together they come to £5,746,878:4s. sterling. The account of what was imported under register, he assures us, is exact. He gives the details of the particular places the gold and silver came from, and of the particular quantity of each metal that, according to the register, each of them supplied. He also makes an allowance for the quantity of each metal that he supposes may have been smuggled. The great experience of this judicious merchant gives his opinion considerable weight.

Cited (via the Postscript to the Universal Merchant) for the estimated ratio of gold to silver annually imported into Europe.

## modern-en · ruddiman · 11:120

In an ancient manuscript of the Regiam Majestatem, an old Scottish law book, there is a statute of assize in which the price of bread is regulated according to all the different prices of wheat, from tenpence to three shillings the Scotch boll, equal to about half an English quarter. Mr Ruddiman seems (see his Preface to Anderson's Diplomata Scotiae) to conclude from this that three shillings was the highest price wheat ever reached in those days, and that tenpence, a shilling, or at most two shillings were the ordinary prices. But on consulting the manuscript it is plainly clear that all these prices are set down only as examples of the proportion to be kept between the respective prices of wheat and bread. The statute's last words are "reliqua judicabis secundum praescripta, habendo respectum ad pretium bladi": "You shall judge the remaining cases according to what is written above, having regard to the price of corn."

## modern-en · fleetwood · 11:115

First, in ancient times almost all rents were paid in kind, in a certain quantity of corn, cattle, poultry, and so on. Sometimes, however, the landlord would stipulate that he should be free to demand of the tenant either the annual payment in kind or a fixed sum of money instead. The price at which the payment in kind was thus exchanged for a fixed sum of money is called in Scotland the conversion price. Since the landlord always has the choice of taking either the goods or the price, it is necessary, for the tenant's safety, that the conversion price should be below rather than above the average market price. In many places, accordingly, it is not much above half this price. Through most of Scotland this custom still holds for poultry, and in some places for cattle. It would probably have held for corn too, had the institution of the public fiars not put an end to it. The fiars are annual valuations, by the judgment of an assize, of the average price of every different sort and quality of grain, according to the actual market price in each different county. This institution made it safe enough for the tenant, and far more convenient for the landlord, to convert, as they call it, the corn rent at whatever happened to be the fiars price of each year, rather than at any fixed price. But the writers who have collected the prices of corn in ancient times seem often to have mistaken what is called in Scotland the conversion price for the actual market price. Fleetwood admits on one occasion that he had made this mistake. But because he wrote his book for a particular purpose, he does not see fit to make this admission until after copying out this conversion price fifteen times. The price is eight shillings the quarter of wheat. This sum in 1423, the year he begins with, contained the same quantity of silver as sixteen shillings of our present money. But in 1562, the year he ends with, it contained no more than the same nominal sum does now.

Collected historical wheat prices from 1202 to 1597 (in his Chronicon Preciosum), and acknowledged, on one occasion, having mistaken a conversion price for an actual market price.

## modern-en · gregory-king · 8:32

Over the course of the last century, taking one year with another, grain was dearer in both parts of the United Kingdom than during the present one. This is a fact that can no longer be reasonably doubted, and the proof is, if anything, even more decisive for Scotland than for England. In Scotland it rests on the public fiars — annual valuations, made on oath according to the actual state of the markets, of every sort of grain in every county of Scotland. If such direct proof needed any backing, I would add that the same has held in France, and probably in most of Europe. For France the proof is perfectly clear. But while it is certain that in both parts of the United Kingdom grain was somewhat dearer in the last century than now, it is equally certain that labour was much cheaper. So if the labouring poor could raise their families then, they must be much more comfortable now. In the last century the most usual day-wages for common labour across most of Scotland were sixpence in summer and fivepence in winter. Three shillings a week — very nearly the same rate — is still paid in parts of the Highlands and Western Isles. Across most of the Lowlands the most usual wage for common labour is now eightpence a day; tenpence, sometimes a shilling, around Edinburgh, in the counties bordering England — probably because of that nearness — and in a few other places where the demand for labour has lately risen considerably, around Glasgow, Carron, Ayrshire, and so on. In England, improvements in farming, manufacturing, and trade began much earlier than in Scotland. The demand for labour, and hence its price, must have risen with them. So in the last century as in the present, wages were higher in England than in Scotland. They have also risen considerably since then, though, because of the greater variety of rates paid in different places, it is harder to say by how much. In 1614 a foot soldier's pay was the same as today, eightpence a day. When the rate was first set, it would naturally have been pegged to the usual wage of common labourers, the class from which foot soldiers are usually drawn. Lord Chief Justice Hales, writing in the reign of Charles II, reckons the necessary cost of a labourer's family of six — father, mother, two children able to do something, and two who cannot — at ten shillings a week, or twenty-six pounds a year. If they cannot earn this by their labour, he supposes, they must make it up by begging or stealing. He seems to have looked into the matter very carefully. (See his scheme for the maintenance of the poor in Burn's History of the Poor Laws.) In 1688 Mr Gregory King, whose skill in political arithmetic Dr Davenant praises so highly, reckoned the ordinary income of labourers and out-servants at fifteen pounds a year per family, which he took to consist on average of three and a half persons. So his figure, though it looks different, comes very nearly to the same thing at bottom as Judge Hales's. Both put the weekly cost of such families at about twentypence a head. Both the cash income and the cash expense of such families have risen considerably since then through most of the kingdom — more in some places, less in others — though perhaps almost nowhere as much as some exaggerated accounts of present wages have lately led the public to believe. It must be noted that the price of labour can nowhere be pinned down very precisely, since different rates are often paid in the same place for the same kind of work, not only according to the worker's differing skill but according to the easiness or harshness of the employer. Where wages are not fixed by law, all we can claim to determine is what the most usual rates are; and experience seems to show that the law can never set them properly, however often it has pretended to.

"A man famous for his knowledge in matters of this kind," who in 1688 estimated the average produce of English land and the ordinary income of labourers and out-servants.

## modern-en · john-smith-wool · 28:24

An earlier part of this work has shown that the effect of these regulations has been to depress the price of English wool not only below what it would naturally be today, but far below what it actually was in the time of Edward III. Scotch wool, once it came under the same regulations through the Union, is said to have fallen by about half. The very accurate and intelligent author of the Memoirs of Wool, the Reverend Mr John Smith, observes that the price of the best English wool in England is generally below what wool of much lower quality usually fetches in the Amsterdam market. The avowed purpose of these regulations was to push the price of this commodity below what might be called its natural and proper level, and there seems no doubt they produced the effect expected of them.

Author of the Memoirs of Wool, cited for the historical price of English wool -- explicitly named as "the Reverend Mr John Smith," distinct from Adam Smith himself.

## modern-en · douglas · 11:43

In Virginia and Maryland, growing tobacco is preferred to growing corn as the more profitable. Tobacco could be grown with advantage over most of Europe, but in almost every part of Europe it has become a main object of taxation; and collecting a tax from every separate farm in the country where the plant might happen to be grown has been thought more difficult than levying one on its import at the custom-house. For this reason the growing of tobacco has been most absurdly prohibited over most of Europe, which necessarily gives a sort of monopoly to the countries where it is allowed; and since Virginia and Maryland produce the most of it, they share largely, though with some rivals, in the advantage of this monopoly. Growing tobacco, however, seems not to be as profitable as growing sugar. I have never even heard of a tobacco plantation improved and cultivated by the capital of merchants living in Great Britain, and our tobacco colonies send us home no such wealthy planters as we often see arrive from our sugar islands. Although the preference given in those colonies to tobacco over corn suggests that Europe's effective demand for tobacco is not fully supplied, it is probably more nearly supplied than the demand for sugar; and although the present price of tobacco is probably more than enough to pay the whole rent, wages, and profit needed to prepare it and bring it to market at the rate commonly paid on corn land, it must not be so much more as the present price of sugar is. Accordingly, our tobacco planters have shown the same fear of a glut of tobacco that the owners of the old vineyards in France have of a glut of wine. By act of assembly they have limited its cultivation to six thousand plants, supposed to yield a thousandweight of tobacco, for every negro between sixteen and sixty years old. Such a negro, over and above this quantity of tobacco, can manage, they reckon, four acres of Indian corn. To keep the market from being overstocked, they have also sometimes, in plentiful years, burned a certain quantity of tobacco for every negro, as the Dutch are said to do with spices, so Dr Douglas tells us in his Summary, vol. ii, pp. 372, 373, though I suspect he was ill informed. If such violent methods are needed to keep up the present price of tobacco, the advantage of growing it over growing corn, if it still has any, will probably not last long.

Author of a Summary Smith cites, with some suspicion of misinformation, on plentiful herring catches.

## modern-en · arbuthnot · 29:47

The policy of the ancient republics of Greece, and that of Rome, honoured agriculture above manufactures and foreign trade, but it seems to have discouraged those latter occupations rather than to have given agriculture any direct or deliberate encouragement. In several of the ancient Greek states foreign trade was banned outright; in several others the trades of craftsmen and manufacturers were held to harm the strength and agility of the body, to spoil it for the habits their military and gymnastic exercises tried to instil, and so to make it more or less unfit for bearing the hardships and facing the dangers of war. Such occupations were thought fit only for slaves, and the free citizens were forbidden to practise them. Even in states where there was no such ban, as in Rome and Athens, the bulk of the people were in effect shut out of all the trades now commonly carried on by the lower townsfolk. At Athens and Rome those trades were all worked by the slaves of the rich, who carried them on for their masters' profit; the masters' wealth, power, and protection made it nearly impossible for a poor freeman to find a market for his work when it had to compete with the work of the rich men's slaves. But slaves are very seldom inventive, and all the most important improvements in machinery, or in the arrangement and distribution of work, that make labour easier and quicker have been the discoveries of free men. If a slave were to suggest any such improvement, his master would very likely treat the suggestion as a sign of laziness and a wish to spare himself work at the master's expense. Instead of a reward, the poor slave would probably meet plenty of abuse and perhaps some punishment. So in manufactures run by slaves, more labour must generally have been spent to do the same amount of work than in those run by free men, and the slave's work must therefore generally have cost more than the free man's. Mr Montesquieu remarks that the Hungarian mines, though no richer, have always been worked at less expense, and so at more profit, than the Turkish mines nearby. The Turkish mines are worked by slaves, and those slaves' arms are the only machines the Turks have ever thought to use; the Hungarian mines are worked by free men, who employ a great deal of machinery to make their own labour easier and quicker. From the little we know about the price of manufactures in Greek and Roman times, the finer sorts appear to have been extremely dear. Silk sold for its weight in gold. It was not then a European manufacture, and since it all came from the East Indies, the distance of carriage may partly explain the high price. But the price a lady would sometimes pay, it is said, for a piece of very fine linen seems just as extravagant; and since linen was always a European, or at most an Egyptian, manufacture, this high price can only be explained by the great cost of the labour that went into it—and that cost of labour could come from nothing but the clumsiness of the machinery used. The price of fine woollens, too, though not quite so extravagant, seems to have been well above today's. Pliny tells us (Plin. l. ix, c. 39) that some cloths dyed in a particular way cost a hundred denarii, or £3 6s 8d, the pound weight, and others dyed another way cost a thousand denarii the pound weight, or £33 6s 8d. Remember that the Roman pound held only twelve of our avoirdupois ounces. This high price does seem to have been chiefly due to the dye. But unless the cloths themselves had been much dearer than any made today, so costly a dye would probably not have been used on them; the gap between the value of the accessory and that of the main item would have been too great. The price the same author mentions (Plin. l. viii, c. 48) for certain triclinaria—a kind of woollen pillow or cushion used for leaning on while reclining on couches at table—beggars belief; some are said to have cost more than £30,000, others more than £300,000, and in this case the high price is not put down to the dye. Dr Arbuthnot observes that the dress of fashionable people of both sexes seems to have shown much less variety in ancient than in modern times, and the very limited variety we see in ancient statues bears him out. He concludes that their dress must on the whole have been cheaper than ours, but the conclusion does not seem to follow. When fashionable dress is very expensive, the variety must be very small; but when improvements in manufacturing make the cost of any one outfit quite moderate, the variety naturally becomes very great. The rich, unable to set themselves apart by the cost of any single outfit, will naturally try to do so by the number and variety of their outfits.

Observed that there was much less variety of dress and household furniture in ancient than in modern times.

## modern-en · swift · 31:181

The high duties imposed on the import of many different kinds of foreign goods, meant to discourage their consumption in Great Britain, have in many cases done nothing but encourage smuggling, and in all cases have brought in less customs revenue than more moderate duties would have yielded. Dr Swift's remark—that in the arithmetic of the customs, two and two sometimes make only one instead of four—holds perfectly true of such heavy duties, which could never have been imposed had the mercantile system not taught us, in many cases, to use taxation as a tool not of revenue but of monopoly.

Author of the saying that in the arithmetic of the customs, two and two do not always make four but sometimes only one -- an illustration of how excessive duties can reduce revenue by encouraging smuggling.

## modern-en · ramazzini · 8:42

Generous pay for labour, just as it encourages people to have children, also raises the industry of the common people. Wages are the spur to industry, which, like every other human quality, improves in proportion to the encouragement it gets. Plentiful food increases the worker's bodily strength, and the comforting hope of bettering his condition — of ending his days, perhaps, in ease and plenty — drives him to exert that strength to the utmost. Where wages are high, accordingly, we always find workers more active, diligent, and quick than where wages are low: in England, for instance, more than in Scotland; near great towns more than in remote country places. Some workers, it is true, when they can earn in four days enough to keep them through the week, will be idle the other three. This, however, is by no means true of most of them. On the contrary, workers paid generously by the piece are very apt to overwork themselves and ruin their health within a few years. A carpenter in London, and in some other places, is reckoned not to last at his full vigour above eight years. Something of the same happens in many other piecework trades — as most manufacturing trades are, and even country labour wherever wages are higher than usual. Almost every class of craftsmen suffers some particular ailment brought on by overdoing their particular kind of work. Ramazzini, an eminent Italian physician, has written a whole book on such diseases. We do not count our soldiers the most industrious people among us; yet when soldiers have been set to certain kinds of work and paid generously by the piece, their officers have often had to make the contractor agree that they should not be allowed to earn above a fixed daily sum at the rate they were paid. Until this was stipulated, rivalry and the desire for greater pay often drove them to overwork and harm their health by excessive labour. Excessive effort during four days of the week is often the real cause of the idleness of the other three, so loudly complained of. Great effort, of mind or body, kept up for several days running is, in most men, naturally followed by a strong craving for rest, which, unless held back by force or some pressing need, is all but irresistible. It is the call of nature, demanding relief through some indulgence — sometimes mere rest, but sometimes too dissipation and amusement. If it is not heeded, the consequences are often dangerous and sometimes fatal, and almost always, sooner or later, bring on the disease peculiar to the trade. If employers would always heed the dictates of reason and humanity, they would often have cause to moderate rather than spur on the effort of many of their workers. It will be found, I believe, in every trade, that the man who works moderately enough to be able to work steadily not only keeps his health longest but, over the course of the year, gets the greatest quantity of work done.

"An eminent Italian physician," author of a book on the occupational diseases peculiar to different trades.

## modern-en · peter-kalm · 11:203

In all new colonies, the great quantity of waste land that for many years can be used for nothing but the feeding of cattle soon makes them extremely abundant; and in everything great cheapness is the necessary result of great abundance. Though all the cattle of the European colonies in America were originally brought from Europe, they soon multiplied so much there, and became of so little value, that even horses were allowed to run wild in the woods without any owner thinking it worth his while to claim them. It must be a long time after the first founding of such colonies before it becomes profitable to feed cattle on the produce of cultivated land. The same causes, then—the want of manure and the mismatch between the stock used in cultivation and the land it is meant to cultivate—are likely to introduce there a system of husbandry not unlike that which still goes on in so many parts of Scotland. Mr Kalm, the Swedish traveller, in his account of the husbandry of some of the English colonies in North America as he found it in 1749, accordingly remarks that he can hardly discover there the character of the English nation, so well skilled in all the different branches of agriculture. They make scarcely any manure for their cornfields, he says; instead, when one piece of ground has been exhausted by continual cropping, they clear and cultivate another piece of fresh land, and when that is exhausted they go on to a third. Their cattle are allowed to wander through the woods and other uncultivated ground, where they are half-starved, having long ago wiped out almost all the annual grasses by cropping them too early in the spring, before they had time to flower or shed their seeds (Kalm's Travels, vol. 1, pp. 343, 344). The annual grasses, it seems, were the best natural grasses in that part of North America, and when the Europeans first settled there they used to grow very thick and rise three or four feet high. A piece of ground that, when he wrote, could not maintain one cow would in former times, he was assured, have maintained four, each of which would have given four times the milk that one was capable of giving. The poorness of the pasture had, in his opinion, caused the degradation of their cattle, which degenerated noticeably from one generation to the next. They were probably not unlike that stunted breed common all over Scotland thirty or forty years ago, which is now so much improved through most of the low country—not so much by changing the breed, though that has been done in some places, as by a more plentiful method of feeding them.

The Swedish traveller, cited on the state of husbandry in some of the English colonies he observed.

## modern-en · buffon · 11:208

The hog, which finds its food among filth and greedily devours many things rejected by every other useful animal, is, like poultry, originally kept as a save-all. As long as the number of such animals that can be reared at little or no expense is fully enough to supply the demand, this kind of butcher's meat comes to market at a much lower price than any other. But when the demand rises beyond what this quantity can supply, when it becomes necessary to raise food on purpose for feeding and fattening hogs, just as for other cattle, the price necessarily rises, and becomes either higher or lower than that of other butcher's meat according as the nature of the country and the state of its agriculture happen to make the feeding of hogs more or less expensive than that of other cattle. In France, according to Mr Buffon, the price of pork is nearly equal to that of beef. In most parts of Great Britain it is at present somewhat higher.

Cited for the relative price of pork and beef in France.

## modern-en · frezier · 11:77

In the silver mines of Peru, Frezier and Ulloa tell us, the owner often demands nothing from the person working the mine except that he grind his ore at the owner's mill, paying the usual toll or grinding fee. Until 1736, indeed, the king of Spain's tax came to a fifth of the standard silver, and until then this fifth could be regarded as the real rent of most of Peru's silver mines, the richest the world has known. Had there been no tax, this fifth would naturally have gone to the landlord, and many mines could have been worked that were not worked at the time because they could not cover the tax. The duke of Cornwall's tax on tin is reckoned at more than five per cent, or a twentieth of the value; and whatever his share, it too would naturally go to the owner of the mine if tin were duty-free. But if you add a twentieth to a sixth, you find that the whole average rent of the tin mines of Cornwall stood to the whole average rent of the silver mines of Peru as thirteen to twelve. Yet the silver mines of Peru can no longer pay even this low rent, and in 1736 the tax on silver was cut from a fifth to a tenth. Even this lighter tax on silver tempts smuggling more than the twentieth on tin does, and smuggling must be far easier with a precious commodity than with a bulky one. So the king of Spain's tax is said to be very poorly paid, and the duke of Cornwall's very well. Rent, then, probably makes up a larger share of the price of tin at the most productive tin mines than of silver at the most productive silver mines in the world. After the capital used to work these different mines has been replaced, along with its ordinary profit, the remainder left to the owner is evidently greater with the coarse metal than with the precious one.

Cited, with Ulloa, on the taxation and rarity of profitable silver mining in Peru; visited Peru in 1713 and described Lima's population.

## modern-en · ulloa · 11:17

But the relative values of these two kinds of food, bread and butcher's meat, differ greatly at different stages of agriculture. In its crude beginnings, the unimproved wilds, which then make up by far the greater part of the country, are all given over to cattle. There is more butcher's meat than bread, so bread is the food most competed for and consequently fetches the highest price. At Buenos Aires, Ulloa tells us, four reals, twenty-one and a half pence sterling, was forty or fifty years ago the usual price of an ox chosen from a herd of two or three hundred. He says nothing about the price of bread, probably because he found nothing remarkable about it. An ox there, he says, costs little more than the labour of catching it. But corn cannot be grown anywhere without a great deal of labour, and in a country lying on the River Plate, at that time the direct route from Europe to the silver mines of Potosi, the money price of labour could be very cheap. It is otherwise once cultivation has spread over the greater part of the country. Then there is more bread than butcher's meat. The competition shifts direction, and the price of butcher's meat comes to exceed that of bread.

Cited, with Frezier, on Peruvian silver mining, and separately for cattle and horse prices at Buenos Aires; resided in the region between 1740 and 1746.

## modern-en · solorzano · 11:164

The king of Spain's tax was cut to a fifth of the registered silver in 1504 (Solorzano, vol. ii), forty-one years before 1545, the date the mines of Potosi were discovered. In the ninety years that followed, before 1636, these mines—the most fertile in all America—had time enough to produce their full effect, that is, to drive the value of silver in the European market as low as it could well fall while still paying this tax to the king of Spain. Ninety years is enough time to bring any commodity that is not under monopoly down to its natural price, or to the lowest price at which, while paying a particular tax, it can keep selling for any considerable stretch of time.

Cited for the reduction of Spain's registered silver coinage to a fifth of its former amount by 1504.

## modern-en · tavernier · 11:84

The demand for precious stones arises entirely from their beauty. They are of no use except as ornaments, and the merit of their beauty is greatly heightened by their scarcity, or by the difficulty and expense of getting them out of the mine. So wages and profit make up almost the whole of their high price on most occasions. Rent comes in for only a very small share, often for none at all, and only the most productive mines yield any considerable rent. When the jeweller Tavernier visited the diamond mines of Golconda and Visiapour, he was told that the country's ruler, for whose benefit they were worked, had ordered all of them shut except those yielding the largest and finest stones. The rest, it seems, were not worth working for the owner.

"A jeweller," who visited the diamond mines of Golconda and Visiapour and reported on their profitability.

## modern-en · borlase · 11:76

A sixth of the gross output may be taken as the average rent of the tin mines of Cornwall, the most productive known anywhere in the world, according to the Reverend Mr Borlace, vice-warden of the stannaries. Some mines, he says, yield more, and some yield less. A sixth of the gross output is also the rent of several highly productive lead mines in Scotland.

Vice-warden of the stannaries, cited on the productivity of some of the world's most fertile tin mines.

## modern-en · machiavelli · 19:19

Sometimes they were introduced in the way described above, by the forceful action, so to speak, of the capital of particular merchants and entrepreneurs who set them up in imitation of some foreign manufacture of the same kind. Such manufactures are therefore the offspring of foreign commerce, and of this kind seem to have been the old manufactures of silks, velvets, and brocades that flourished in Lucca during the thirteenth century. They were driven out of there by the tyranny of one of Machiavelli's heroes, Castruccio Castracani. In 1310 nine hundred families were expelled from Lucca, of whom thirty-one moved to Venice and offered to introduce the silk manufacture there. {See Sandi Istoria civile de Vinezia, part 2 vol. i, page 247 and 256.} Their offer was accepted, many privileges were granted to them, and they began the manufacture with three hundred workmen. Of this kind, too, seem to have been the fine-cloth manufactures that once flourished in Flanders and were brought into England early in Elizabeth's reign, and so are the present silk manufactures of Lyons and Spitalfields. Manufactures introduced in this way generally work foreign materials, being imitations of foreign manufactures. When the Venetian manufacture was first set up, the materials all came from Sicily and the Levant. The older manufacture of Lucca was likewise carried on with foreign materials. The growing of mulberry trees and the breeding of silkworms do not seem to have been common in northern Italy before the sixteenth century, and these arts were not introduced into France until the reign of Charles IX. The manufactures of Flanders were carried on chiefly with Spanish and English wool. Spanish wool was the material not of the first woollen manufacture of England, but of the first that was fit for distant sale. More than half the materials of the Lyons manufacture are foreign silk even today; when it was first established the whole, or very nearly the whole, was foreign. No part of the materials of the Spitalfields manufacture is ever likely to be produced in England. Because such manufactures are generally introduced by the scheme and project of a few individuals, their location is sometimes set in a maritime city and sometimes in an inland town, according to where the founders' interest, judgment, or whim happens to settle.

Cited (as printed, "Machiavel") for the tyranny that banished Castruccio Castracani's silk-weaving families from Lucca, for the revival of religious credit by the mendicant orders, and for the trading methods of Lorenzo de' Medici's agents.

## modern-en · guicciardini · 20:23

Italy is the only great country in Europe that seems to have been cultivated and improved in every part through foreign commerce and manufactures for distant markets. Before Charles VIII's invasion, Italy, according to Guicciardini, was cultivated no less in its most mountainous and barren parts than in its flattest and most fertile ones. The country's favourable position, and the large number of independent states then existing within it, probably contributed a good deal to this widespread cultivation. It is also possible, despite this sweeping statement by one of the most judicious and cautious of modern historians, that Italy was not at that time any better cultivated than England is now.

Cited for the state of Italian land cultivation before Charles VIII's invasion.

## modern-en · lorenzo-de-medici · 31:8

Princes, however, have often gone into many other commercial ventures, willing, like private individuals, to improve their fortunes by becoming adventurers in the ordinary branches of trade. They have almost never succeeded. The extravagance with which a prince's affairs are always run makes success nearly impossible. A prince's agents treat their master's wealth as inexhaustible: they are careless about what price they buy at, careless about what price they sell at, careless about what it costs to ship his goods from one place to another. These agents often live with a prince's extravagance, and sometimes, in spite of that extravagance and by a clever way of keeping their accounts, they end up acquiring a prince's fortune. According to Machiavelli, this is exactly how the agents of Lorenzo de' Medici, no prince of mean abilities, ran his trade. The republic of Florence was several times forced to pay off the debts their extravagance had run up for him. So he found it convenient to give up the merchant's business, the very business to which his family had originally owed their fortune, and in the later part of his life to spend what was left of that fortune, together with the state revenue he controlled, on projects and expenses more befitting his rank.

"Not a prince of mean abilities," whose agents, per Machiavelli, carried on his trade -- an example of princely involvement in commerce.

More than half the materials of the Lyons manufacture are foreign silk even today; when it was first established the whole, or very nearly the whole, was foreign. No part of the materials of the Spitalfields manufacture is ever likely to be produced in England. Because such manufactures are generally introduced by the scheme and project of a few individuals, their location is sometimes set in a maritime city and sometimes in an inland town, according to where the founders' interest, judgment, or whim happens to settle.

Cited (via his Istoria civile di Venezia) for the history of the Venetian silk trade.

## modern-en · pfeffel · 19:6

Nor was that all. At the same time they were generally formed into a community or corporation, with the privilege of having their own magistrates and town council, of making by-laws for their own government, of building walls for their own defence, and of placing all their inhabitants under a kind of military discipline by requiring them to watch and ward—that is, in the old sense, to guard and defend those walls against every attack and surprise, by night as well as by day. In England they were generally exempt from attendance at the hundred and county courts, and any cases arising among them, except pleas of the crown, were left to their own magistrates to decide. In other countries they were often granted much greater and more extensive jurisdictions. {See Madox, Firma Burgi. See also Pfeffel in the Remarkable events under Frederick II. and his Successors of the House of Suabia.}

Cited on remarkable events under Frederick II and his successors of the House of Swabia, and on the early formidable rise of the Hanseatic league.

## modern-en · madox · 10:72

It is to prevent this fall in price, and so in wages and profit, by suppressing the free competition that would certainly cause it, that all corporations and most corporation laws have been set up. In earlier times, in many parts of Europe, no authority was needed to create a corporation except that of the incorporated town where it was established. In England, admittedly, a charter from the king was also required. But this royal prerogative seems to have been kept up more as a way of squeezing money out of subjects than as a defence of common liberty against such oppressive monopolies. On payment of a fine to the king, the charter was generally granted readily enough; and when a particular group of craftsmen or traders chose to act as a corporation without a charter, such bodies, called adulterine guilds, were not always stripped of their privileges on that account, but were merely required to pay the king an annual fine for permission to exercise their usurped rights (see Madox, Firma Burgi, p. 26 and following). Direct oversight of all corporations, and of any bye-laws they chose to enact for their own governance, belonged to the incorporated town where they were based; and whatever discipline was applied to them came, as a rule, not from the king, but from that larger incorporated body of which these lesser ones were only parts or members.

Author of Firma Burgi and a History of the Exchequer, cited repeatedly on medieval English town charters and taxation.

## modern-en · brady · 19:2

They do indeed seem to have been a very poor and lowly set of people, travelling about with their goods from place to place and from fair to fair, like the hawkers and pedlars of today. In every country of Europe back then, just as in several of the Tartar governments of Asia today, taxes were levied on travellers and their goods: when they passed through certain manors, when they crossed certain bridges, when they carried their goods around a fair, and when they set up a booth or stall to sell them. In England these various taxes were called passage, pontage, lastage, and stallage. Sometimes the king, and sometimes a great lord who apparently had authority to do so on certain occasions, would grant particular traders, especially those living on their own demesnes, a general exemption from such taxes. Such traders, though otherwise of servile or nearly servile status, were on this account called free traders. In return they usually paid their protector a kind of annual poll tax. In those days protection was rarely granted without something of value in return, and this tax may perhaps be seen as compensation for whatever their patrons lost by exempting them from other taxes. At first both the poll taxes and the exemptions seem to have been entirely personal, affecting only particular individuals during their own lifetimes or for as long as their protectors chose. In the very incomplete records published from the Domesday Book about several English towns, there is frequent mention of the tax that particular burghers each paid, either to the king or to some other great lord, for this kind of protection, and sometimes only of the total sum of all those taxes. {see Brady's Historical Treatise of Cities and Boroughs, p. 3. etc.}

Author of a Historical Treatise of Cities and Boroughs, cited on medieval town taxation.

## modern-en · charlevoix · 27:37

The French colony of Canada was, during most of the last century and part of the present one, under the government of an exclusive company. Under such an unfavourable administration its progress was necessarily very slow compared with that of other new colonies; but it became much more rapid once this company was dissolved, after the collapse of what is called the Mississippi scheme. When the English took possession of this country, they found in it nearly double the number of inhabitants that father Charlevoix had assigned to it twenty or thirty years before. That Jesuit had travelled over the whole country and had no inclination to make it out as less important than it really was.

Cited for an earlier population estimate of a region later found, by Smith's own account, to hold nearly double the number of inhabitants.

## modern-en · gemelli-careri · 27:31

From the moment its colonies were first established, the crown of Spain drew some revenue from them through its share of the gold and silver. It was also a revenue of a kind to excite in human greed the most extravagant expectation of still greater riches. So the Spanish colonies, from the moment they were first established, drew a great deal of attention from their mother country, while those of the other European nations were for a long time largely neglected. The former perhaps did not thrive any better because of this attention, nor the latter any worse because of this neglect. In proportion to the extent of the country they to some degree occupy, the Spanish colonies are reckoned less populous and thriving than those of almost any other European nation. Even so, the Spanish colonies' progress in population and improvement has certainly been very rapid and very great. The city of Lima, founded since the conquest, is described by Ulloa as containing fifty thousand inhabitants nearly thirty years ago. Quito, which had been only a miserable hamlet of Indians, is described by the same author as being equally populous in his own time. Gemelli Careri, a supposed traveller it is said, but who seems everywhere to have written from very good information, describes the city of Mexico as containing a hundred thousand inhabitants, a number which, despite all the exaggerations of the Spanish writers, is probably more than five times what it held in the time of Montezuma. These figures greatly exceed those of Boston, New York, and Philadelphia, the three largest cities of the English colonies. Before the Spanish conquest there were no draught animals fit for hauling in either Mexico or Peru. The lama was their only beast of burden, and its strength seems to have been a good deal less than that of a common ass. The plough was unknown to them. They had no knowledge of iron. They had no coined money, nor any established medium of trade of any kind. Their commerce was carried on by barter. A kind of wooden spade was their main farming tool. Sharp stones served them as knives and hatchets to cut with; fish bones and the hard sinews of certain animals served them as needles to sew with; and these seem to have been their main tools of trade. In such a state of things, it seems impossible that either of these empires could have been as improved or as well cultivated as it is now, when they are plentifully supplied with all kinds of European livestock, and when the use of iron, the plough, and many of the arts of Europe have been introduced among them. But the populousness of any country must be in proportion to the degree of its improvement and cultivation. Despite the cruel destruction of the natives that followed the conquest, these two great empires are probably more populous now than they ever were before; and the people are surely very different, for we must admit, I think, that the Spanish creoles are in many respects superior to the ancient Indians.

"A pretended traveller, it is said," whose population estimate for a region Smith nonetheless notes agrees with another author's contemporary account.

## modern-en · bouchaud · 31:120

The vicesima hereditatum, the twentieth penny on inheritances that Augustus imposed on the ancient Romans, was a tax on the transfer of property from the dead to the living. Dion Cassius (Book 55; see also Burman, de Vectigalibus Pop. Rom., chapter 11, and Bouchaud, de l'impôt du vingtième sur les successions), the author who describes it least obscurely, says it was charged on all inheritances, legacies, and gifts made at death, except for those passing to the closest relatives and to the poor.

Cited (via his work on the French vingtième tax on inheritances) alongside Dion Cassius and Burman on Roman inheritance taxation.
