## modern-en · expilly · 31:235

The French system of taxation seems in every respect inferior to the British. In Great Britain, ten million pounds sterling are collected each year from fewer than eight million people, without its being possible to say that any particular order is oppressed. From the Collections of the Abbé Expilly, and from the observations of the author of the Essay upon the Legislation and Commerce of Corn, it appears probable that France, including the provinces of Lorraine and Bar, contains about twenty-three or twenty-four million people—three times the number, perhaps, of Great Britain. The soil and climate of France are better than those of Great Britain. The country has been much longer in a state of improvement and cultivation, and is on that account better stocked with all those things that take a long time to raise and accumulate—great towns, and convenient, well-built houses, both in town and country. With these advantages, one might expect that in France a revenue of thirty million could be collected for the support of the state with as little inconvenience as a revenue of ten million is in Great Britain. In 1765 and 1766, the whole revenue paid into the treasury of France, according to the best though, I admit, very imperfect accounts I could get of it, usually ran between 308 and 325 million livres—that is, it did not amount to fifteen million sterling, not half of what might have been expected had the people contributed in the same proportion to their numbers as the people of Great Britain. Yet the people of France, it is generally acknowledged, are much more oppressed by taxes than the people of Great Britain. France, however, is certainly the great empire in Europe which, after Great Britain, enjoys the mildest and most indulgent government.

Cited (via his Collections) on evidence for how a particular order of French taxation fell unevenly on different groups.

## modern-en · barretti · 26:8

Yet it has been hailed as a masterpiece of English commercial policy. Each year Portugal receives from Brazil more gold than its domestic economy can absorb, whether as coin or as plate. The surplus is too valuable to sit idle and locked away in vaults, and since it can find no profitable use at home, it has to be sent abroad, despite any ban, and exchanged for something more useful at home. A large share of it comes to England every year, paid either for English goods or for the goods of other European nations that take their returns through England. Mr Barretti was told that the weekly packet-boat from Lisbon carries, on average, more than £50,000 in gold to England each week. That figure was probably inflated. It would come to more than £2,600,000 a year, which is more than Brazil is thought to supply.

Reported on the weekly packet-boat trade in coin between Portugal and England.

## modern-en · cassendi · 30:237

In countries where most church benefices are very modest, a university chair is generally a better position than a church benefice. In that case the universities have the pick of all the churchmen of the country, who in every country make up by far the most numerous class of men of letters. Where many of the church benefices, on the contrary, are very considerable, the church naturally draws away from the universities most of their eminent men of letters, who generally find some patron who does himself credit by getting them church preferment. In the first case we are likely to find the universities filled with the most eminent men of letters in the country. In the second we are likely to find few eminent men among them, and those few among the youngest members of the body, who are likely, too, to be drained away from it before they can have gained enough experience and knowledge to be of much use to it. Voltaire notes that Father Porée, a Jesuit of no great standing in the republic of letters, was the only professor France ever had whose works were worth reading. In a country that has produced so many eminent men of letters, it must seem rather odd that scarcely one of them should have been a university professor. The famous Gassendi was, early in his life, a professor at the University of Aix. When his genius first began to show, it was pointed out to him that by going into the church he could easily find a much quieter and more comfortable living, as well as a better situation for pursuing his studies; and he at once took the advice. Voltaire's observation may, I believe, be applied not only to France but to all other Roman Catholic countries. We very rarely find in any of them an eminent man of letters who is a university professor, except perhaps in the professions of law and medicine, professions from which the church is not so likely to draw them away. After the Church of Rome, that of England is by far the richest and best-endowed church in Christendom. In England, accordingly, the church is continually draining the universities of all their best and ablest members; and an old college tutor known and distinguished across Europe as an eminent man of letters is as rarely to be found there as in any Roman Catholic country. In Geneva, on the contrary, in the Protestant cantons of Switzerland, in the Protestant countries of Germany, in Holland, in Scotland, in Sweden, and in Denmark, the most eminent men of letters those countries have produced have—not all, indeed, but the far greater part of them—been university professors. In those countries the universities are continually draining the church of all its most eminent men of letters.

"The famous Cassendi," who was, early in his life, a professor at a university -- an example against the claim that no eminent philosopher of the age held such a post.

## modern-en · hawkins · 28:18

For the honour of our national humanity, one hopes neither of these statutes was ever carried out. The first, however, has so far as I know never been directly repealed, and Serjeant Hawkins seems to treat it as still in force. The legal writer who still considered a particular old statute in force, though Smith notes it had never been directly repealed.

## modern-en · drummond · 5:29

As a result of any change, however, in this regulated ratio, this distinction becomes—or at least seems to become—something more than nominal again. If the regulated value of a guinea, for example, were either reduced to twenty or raised to twenty-two shillings, then, since all accounts are kept and almost all debt obligations expressed in silver money, the greater part of payments could in either case be made with the same quantity of silver money as before, but would require very different quantities of gold money—more in the one case, less in the other. Silver would appear more invariable in its value than gold. Silver would appear to measure the value of gold, and gold would not appear to measure the value of silver. The value of gold would seem to depend on the quantity of silver it would exchange for, and the value of silver would not seem to depend on the quantity of gold it would exchange for. This difference, however, would be entirely due to the custom of keeping accounts, and of expressing the amount of all sums large and small, in silver rather than in gold money. One of Mr Drummond's notes for twenty-five or fifty guineas would, after an alteration of this kind, still be payable with twenty-five or fifty guineas, just as before. After such an alteration it would be payable with the same quantity of gold as before, but with very different quantities of silver. In the payment of such a note, gold would appear more invariable in its value than silver. Gold would appear to measure the value of silver, and silver would not appear to measure the value of gold. If the custom of keeping accounts, and of expressing promissory notes and other money obligations, in this manner were ever to become general, gold, and not silver, would be regarded as the metal that was particularly the standard or measure of value.

The banker whose promissory notes for twenty-five or fifty guineas are used to illustrate how a change in the gold-silver coin ratio would affect payment in one metal versus the other.

## modern-en · cromwell · 27:112

It should be noted that England was a great trading country, with a very large mercantile capital that was likely to grow greater every day, not only before the Act of Navigation had set up the monopoly of the corn trade but before that trade was even very considerable. In the Dutch war, under Cromwell's government, her navy was superior to that of Holland; and in the war that broke out at the start of Charles II's reign, it was at least equal, perhaps superior, to the combined navies of France and Holland. Its superiority would scarcely look any greater in the present day, at least if the Dutch navy now bore the same proportion to Dutch commerce that it did then. But this great naval power could not, in either of those wars, have been owing to the Act of Navigation. During the first war, the plan of that act had only just been drawn up; and though it had been fully enacted by legal authority before the second war broke out, no part of it could have had time to produce any considerable effect, least of all the part that set up the exclusive trade to the colonies. Both the colonies and their trade were small then, compared with what they are now. The island of Jamaica was an unhealthy desert, little inhabited and still less cultivated. New York and New Jersey were in the hands of the Dutch, and half of St Christopher's in those of the French. The island of Antigua, the two Carolinas, Pennsylvania, Georgia, and Nova Scotia had not been settled. Virginia, Maryland, and New England were settled; and although they were very thriving colonies, there was perhaps not at that time, in Europe or America, a single person who foresaw, or even suspected, the rapid progress they have since made in wealth, population, and improvement. In short, the island of Barbados was the only British colony of any importance whose condition then bore any resemblance to what it is now. The trade of the colonies, of which England enjoyed only a part even for some time after the Act of Navigation (for that act was not very strictly enforced until several years after it was passed), could not at that time have been the cause of the great trade of England, nor of the great naval power that trade supported. The trade that supported that great naval power at the time was the trade of Europe and of the Mediterranean countries. But the share of that trade that Great Britain now enjoys could not support any such great naval power. Had the growing trade of the colonies been left open to all nations, then whatever share of it might have fallen to Great Britain, and a very considerable share probably would have, must all have been an addition to the great trade she already had. Because of the monopoly, the increase of the colony trade has not so much added to the trade Great Britain had before as completely changed its direction.

Whose standing army, formed during his government, eventually turned the Long Parliament out of doors -- compared to Julius Caesar's destruction of the Roman republic; his navy is also cited as superior to Holland's during the Anglo-Dutch war of his time.

## modern-en · henry-pelham · 11:160

Between 1741 and 1750, however, the bounty must have kept the price of corn in the home market from falling as low as it naturally would have. The bounty paid on this amounted to £1,514,962:17:4½. Accordingly, in 1749 Mr Pelham, then prime minister, pointed out to the House of Commons that, over the three preceding years, an extraordinary sum had been paid as bounty for exporting corn. He had good reason to say so, and the following year he could have said it with even more force: in that single year the bounty paid came to no less than £324,176:10:6 (see Tracts on the Corn Trade, Tract 3). The famous excise scheme of Sir Robert Walpole aimed to set up, for wine and tobacco, a system not very unlike the one proposed here. ## modern-en · earl-of-chatham · 32:50

Add to this sum the balance of the Earl of Chatham's and Mr Calcraft's accounts, along with other army savings of the same kind, plus what has been received from the Bank, the East India Company, and the extra shilling-in-the-pound land tax, and the whole must come to a good deal more than five millions. So the debt that has been paid off since the peace out of the savings from the ordinary revenue of the state has not, taking one year with another, amounted to half a million a year. The sinking fund has certainly grown a good deal since the peace: the debt that has been paid off, the reduction of the redeemable four per cents to three per cents, and the life annuities that have fallen in have all added to it. If peace were to continue, perhaps a million a year might now be set aside from it towards discharging the debt. A second million was in fact paid during the past year; but at the same time a large civil-list debt was left unpaid, and we are now caught up in a new war that may, as it goes on, prove as costly as any of our earlier ones. {It has proved more expensive than any one of our former wars, and has saddled us with an additional debt of more than a hundred millions. During eleven years of deep peace, little more than ten millions of debt was paid off; during a seven-year war, more than a hundred millions was contracted.} The new debt that will probably be run up before the next campaign ends may come close to equalling all the old debt that has been paid off from savings out of the state's ordinary revenue. It is therefore pure fantasy to expect that the public debt will ever be completely paid off out of any savings likely to be made from that ordinary revenue as it now stands.

William Pitt the Elder, whose army accounts, balanced with Mr. Calcraft's, are cited among wartime savings figures.

## modern-en · cameron-of-lochiel · 20:8

It is a mistake to suppose that these territorial jurisdictions originated in feudal law. Not only the highest civil and criminal jurisdictions, but also the power to raise troops, to coin money, and even to make by-laws governing their own people, were all rights held outright by the great landowners several centuries before the feudal law was even named in Europe. The authority and jurisdiction of the Saxon lords in England seem to have been as great before the Norman Conquest as that of any Norman lords after it, yet feudal law is not thought to have become the common law of England until after the Conquest. That the great lords in France held the most extensive authority and jurisdictions outright, long before feudal law was introduced there, is a fact beyond doubt. That authority and those jurisdictions all flowed necessarily from the state of property and manners I have just described. Without going back to the remote origins of the French or English monarchies, we can find, in much more recent times, plenty of proof that such effects always follow from such causes. Less than thirty years ago, Mr Cameron of Lochiel, a gentleman of Lochaber in Scotland, used to exercise the highest criminal jurisdiction over his own people without any legal warrant whatsoever; he was not what was then called a lord of regality, nor even a tenant-in-chief, but a vassal of the Duke of Argyll, and not even a justice of the peace. He is said to have done so very fairly, though without any of the usual legal formalities, and it is likely that conditions in that part of the country at the time made it necessary for him to take on this authority in order to keep the peace. That gentleman, whose rent never exceeded £500 a year, led 800 of his own people into the rebellion with him in 1745.

A gentleman of Lochaber who, without legal authority or even the rank of tenant-in-chief, exercised a criminal jurisdiction as a vassal of the Duke of Argyll -- cited as a recent (within thirty years) illustration of feudal-style authority persisting in the Scottish Highlands.

## modern-en · peter-the-great · 30:41

Just as a civilized country can be defended only by means of a well-regulated standing army, so it is only by such an army that a barbarous country can be quickly and tolerably civilized. A standing army imposes the sovereign's law with irresistible force throughout the remotest provinces of the empire and keeps up some degree of regular government in countries that could otherwise admit of none. Whoever examines closely the improvements that Peter the Great introduced into the Russian empire will find that they almost all come down to the establishment of a well-regulated standing army. It is the instrument that carries out and maintains all his other measures. The degree of order and internal peace that empire has enjoyed ever since is owing entirely to the influence of that army.

His improvements to the Russian empire, Smith argues, almost all resolve into the establishment of a well-regulated standing army.

## modern-en · abraham · 4:7

The first public stamps of this kind affixed to the metals in circulation seem in many cases to have been intended to certify what was both the hardest and the most important thing to certify—namely, the goodness or fineness of the metal. They seem to have resembled the sterling mark now stamped on silver plate and bars, or the Spanish mark sometimes stamped on ingots of gold, which, being struck on only one side of the piece and not covering the whole surface, certifies the fineness but not the weight of the metal. Abraham weighs out to Ephron the four hundred shekels of silver he had agreed to pay for the field of Machpelah. They are said, however, to be the merchant's current money, and yet they are received by weight, not by count, in just the same way that ingots of gold and bars of silver are at present. The revenues of the ancient Saxon kings of England are said to have been paid not in money but in kind—that is, in food and provisions of all sorts. William the Conqueror introduced the custom of paying them in money. This money, however, was for a long time received at the exchequer by weight, not by count.

Cited (Genesis) for weighing out four hundred shekels of silver to Ephron for the field of Machpelah, an early example of metal exchanged by weight rather than by stamped coin.

## modern-en · esau · 20:15

Once the tenants had become independent in this way and the retainers had been dismissed, the great landowners were no longer able to interrupt the regular working of justice or disturb the country's peace. Having sold their birthright, not like Esau for a bowl of stew in a time of hunger and need, but in the recklessness of plenty for trinkets and baubles fitter to be children's toys than the serious pursuits of grown men, they became as insignificant as any solid townsman or tradesman in a city. A regular government was established in the country just as in the town, since nobody now had enough power to disrupt its workings in either place.

Cited for selling his birthright for a mess of pottage -- an image Smith uses (by explicit contrast) for Scottish landed gentlemen exchanging their feudal power for luxuries, but not under any such pressing necessity.
